On Second Order Phase Transitions
Let us briefly discuss the so called continuous or second order phase transitions,
because it is the theory of continuous phase transitions in statistical mechanics
which is related to (Euclidean) quantum field theory in most close way.
    Consider a magnet, i.e. a piece of a magnetic material. It can be thought
of as consisting of “micromagnets”, i.e. small elements having nonzero magnetic
moments. Although in reality the structure of “micromagnets” is usually rather
complex, for the sake of this discussion we can think of, say, a cubic lattice with
each cell representing a “micromagnet” having a magnetic moment (proportional to
the volume of the cell);


                                       a
                                                 →    a3 · mi



The interaction between the “micromagnets” is such that they tend to align, thus
producing an overall magnetic moment

                                  V M = V ⟨mi ⟩ ,

where M denotes specific (per unit volume) magnetization, and the expectation
value in the r.h.s. is taken over thermal equilibrium state. The thermal fluctuations
tend to put the directions of mi at random, and as the result of this competition the
ferromagnet has two phases, the low temperature, or ordered phase with nonvanish-
ing local magnetization M, and high-temperature disordered phase with M = 0.
    The most important qualitative features of this phase transition, which occurs
at certain critical temperature
                                       T = Tc ,
can be understood through the Landau Theory.

   Landau Theory: Consider the thermodynamic potential

                                    V Ω(M, T )


                                           161
as the function of specific magnetization M and temperature T ; Ω denotes the
specific (per unit volume) thermodynamic potential. The equilibrium value of M is
determined by the equation
                                  ∂Ω(M, T )
                                             =0.
                                      ∂M
   Consider the high-temperature phase T > Tc , where the solution of this equation
is M = 0, and assume that one can expand Ω(M, T ) in powers of M near this
minimum,
                    Ω(M, T ) = A(T ) + B(T ) M2 + C(T ) M4 + · · ·
(I assume here isotropy of the problem, hence the expansion is in powers of M2 ). It
is clear that M = 0 is the right solution (i.e. the minimum of Ω) only as long as

                              B(T ) > 0      →      M = 0.

When B(T ) becomes negative (as it happens at T < Tc , according to this theory),
the minimum occurs at nonzero M such that
                                      r
                                         −B
                               |M| =         ;
                                         2C
in fact there is a continuum of degenerate minima corresponding to different direc-
tions of M. This situation represents the low-temperature phase where spontaneous
magnetization appears. Thus, in the Landau theory the critical temperature Tc is
the point where B(T ) changes its sign,

                               B(T ) > 0          at T > Tc
                               B(T ) < 0          at T < Tc

   Landau have assumed that near Tc one can write B(T ) as

                              B(T ) = b (T − Tc ) + · · · ,

with some positive constant b. Then it follows that
                          |M| = 0                    at T > Tc
                                           1/2
                          |M| ∼ Tc − T               at T < Tc

It is not difficult to calculate the entropy at T ≈ Tc ,
                                            ∂Ω(M, T )
                                S/V = −               ≈
                                               ∂T
                                           162
                               
                                    0                          for T > Tc
                     S0 /V +       b2
                                                 
                                   2C
                                        T − Tc                 for T < Tc
where S0 refers to the critical point. The entropy (and hence the internal energy) is
continuous at Tc , so there is no latent heat. This is the characteristic property of a
second order phase transition. The specific heat
                                                     ∂S
                                          C=T
                                                     ∂T
has finite discontinuity at T = Tc .

   Critical behavior: Although the Landau theory explains existence and the
”continuous” nature of the critical point, in real systems the singularity of the
thermodynamic functions at Tc turns out to be more complicated then the above
arguments predict. Experiment shows that

                          |M| ∼ (Tc − T )β             for T < Tc ,

with β ≈ 0.35 ± 0.03, definitely different from 0.5. The specific heat not only
discontinuous, but in fact diverges near Tc ,

                                        C ∼ |Tc − T |α ,

with α ≈ −0.12, not 0.

    The Landau theory does not take into account prominent role of the long-range
fluctuations of the magnetization near critical point. According to the thermody-
namic theory of fluctuations, the probability to have some non-equilibrium value M
of the magnetization is (for T > Tc )
                                           V Ω(M,T )          V B(T )
                                                                      M2
                           P (M) ∼ e−         kT       ∼ e−     kT



If B > 0, in the limit V → ∞ the fluctuations are totally suppressed. Vanishing
of B(T ) at T = Tc indicates that in the critical domain the fluctuations must play
significant role.

Fluctuation Theory of Phase Transitions
Previously, we have given a brief discussion of critical behavior near a second order
phase transition. We have pointed out singular character of the thermodynamic

                                              163
functions near the critical point, and suggested that the origin of the singularities
is in the enhanced role of fluctuations.
    The qualitative picture of the fluctuations is as follows. In the vicinity of Tc the
competition between the energetic tendency of the micromagnets to align, and the
thermal fluctuations leads to formation of large domains of approximately collinear
micromagnets,




so that if one looks inside one domain the magnets are almost aligned, but their
average directions can be largely different in two distant domains. Near Tc the
average sizes of the domains become much larger then any microscopic scales, and the
domains involve macroscopic numbers of the micromagnets. The orientations of the
domains experience fluctuations which vary slowly in space (in the the microscopic
scale). These long-range fluctuations lead to formation of the critical singularities
at Tc .
    Having this picture in mind, one can consider local magnetization M(x), a slow
varying function of the spatial coordinates (meaning that the distance at which
M(x) changes significantly must involve macroscopic number of the micromagnets).
One then replaces the homogeneous magnetization M by the local magnetization
                                         Z
                       V Ω(M, T ) →         d3 x Ω(M(x), T ) .

Once M is not a constant, one may include the spatial derivatives of M(x), and
assume that the long-range fluctuations have the probability distribution
                                                        
                                    −1          H[M(x)]
                       P [M(x)] ∼ Z     exp −
                                                   kT
with the ”effective energy” of the form
                                 Z
                     H[M(x)] =      d3 x Ω M(x), ∇M(x), ...; T )

                                          164
where Ω now is a local density. In the spirit of the Landau theory we can write
                                                                   2
         Ω = A(T ) + B(T ) M2 (x) + C(T ) M4 (x) + D(T ) ∇M(x) + ...

where the dots may involve higher powers of M2 as well as terms with higher deriva-
tives. Neglecting all but the terms explicitly shown above corresponds to the so-
called Landau-Ginzburg model.
    With this probability distribution, the free energy of the magnet is to be com-
puted as some sort of functional integral
                                     Z
                                F                    1
                              − kT
                        Z=e        =    [DM(x)] e− kT H[M(x)]

where the integration is over the functions M(x) which vary slowly in terms of the
microscopic scale.
   Note that this expression differs mostly in notations from the functional integral
in Euclidean scalar field theory. After renormalization
                              r
                                 2D(T )
                                         M(x) = φ0 (x)
                                   kT
it becomes                            Z
                                Z=        [Dφ0 ] e−A[φ0 ] ,

where in the Landau-Ginzburg approximation

                                         m20 2 λ0 4
                         Z                         
                              1       2
                     A=         (∂φ0 ) +    φ +  φ
                              2          2 0 4! 0
has the familiar form, with

                            m20 = m20 (T ) ,      λ0 = λ0 (T )

some functions of T , whose form depends on microscopic details of the magnetic
system. On the superficial level the main difference is in the dimensionality of the
space (3 instead of 4), and the number of components of the scalar field (3 instead
of 1). As for the difference in d, we have already seen that the idea of studying QFT
in spaces of different (even non-integer) dimensions d is useful. The problematic of
the phase transition theory, in its application to condensed matter physics, usually
corresponds to the case d = 3. But it is interesting and instructive to study criticality
at any d, in particular to see how the characteristics of a phase transition change

                                            165
with d. Also, in the above simple model of magnet, the direction of M is completely
decoupled from any space direction. Therefore formally the dimensionality of the
vector M, or equivalently the number of components of
                                φ0 = {φ0,i } ,    i = 1, 2, ..., N
can be any number, not necessarily d. In fact, there are magnets with anisotropy
of the type of ”easy plane” or ”easy axis”, which correspond to N = 2 and N = 1,
respectively. In most of the following discussion I will assume N = 1 for simplicity
of notations.

   On a deeper level, one can raise two objections against this picture. First, the
above formulation assumes integration only over the ”slow varying” fields φ0 (x).
This is similar to the idea of cutoff in field theory, but here it seems to be dictated
by physics of the problem. Indeed, in our approach the ”thermodynamic potential”
                                         H([M(x)], T )
already takes into account ”microscopic” thermal fluctuations in our system, while
the ”macroscopic” fluctuations are left to be accounted for in the functional integral
over D[M(x)]. But how these two types of fluctuations are separated? We have said
that M(x) can vary on the scale ” much larger then the microscopic scales”. But
how much larger? Should we take the cutoff parameter Λ = (100 a)−1 , or (104 a)−1 ?
The second objection is that it is not clear what makes it possible to neglect the
higher order (both in M and derivatives) terms in H.
    These questions are at the heart of the Wilson’s approach to the Renormalization
Group.

Wilson’s Approach 12
To come to the idea of this approach let us ask instead what difference does it make
if we take
                Λ = Λ0 = (100 a)−1       or     Λ = Λ1 = (104 a)−1 .
In other words, if we consider say φ4 theory, and write down the functional integral
                                 Z
                                   D[φ0 ] e−A[φ0 ] (...)

  12

recommended!). See also L.Kadanoff, ”Statistics, Dynamics, and Renormalization”, WS


                                              166
in its bare form, with some cutoff regularization implied, how the theory depends
on the value of the cutoff parameter Λ?
   Because basic ideas of the Wilson’s RG do not depend very much on precise way
how the cutoff regularization is implemented, let me assume specific one. Let me
assume that the field φ0 can be written as the Fourier-like integral
                                           dd k
                                   Z
                          φ0 (x) =              d
                                                  ϕ(k) eikx ,
                                    |k|<Λ (2π)

corresponds to the cutoff prepagator
                                             Φ(k 2 /Λ2 )
                                  DΛ (k) =
                                             k 2 + m20
with Φ(x) = Θ(1 − x) being the step function. (We did not consider this regu-

diagrams).
    So our question now is how the functional integral depends on Λ, or what is the
difference between the integral with Λ = Λ0 and Λ = Λ1 . Let us temporarily use
the notations
                       Z                             Z
                                    ikx
              φ0 (x) =        ϕ(k) e ,      φ1 (x) =        ϕ(k) eikx .
                        |k|<Λ0                               |k|<Λ1

If Λ0 > Λ1 the difference of course is that the integration over φ0 includes the

                                     Λ0 > |k| > Λ1 ,
while the integral over φ1 does not. We can write
                                 φ0 (x) = φ1 (x) + φ̃(x) ,
where                                  Z
                             φ̃(x) =                ϕ(k) eikx .
                                       Λ0 >|k|>Λ1

In this context, the parts φ1 (x) and φ̃ are often called the ”slow part” and the ”fast
part”, respectively. We have
                                  D[φ0 ] = D[φ1 ]D[φ̃] .

                                           167
   Suppose we want to calculate some correlation function in the theory with the
cutoff Λ0 ,
                                      1
                                         Z
            ⟨ φ0 (x1 )...φ0 (xn ) ⟩ =      D[φ0 ] φ0 (x1 )...φ0 (xn ) e−A0 [φ0 ] ,
                                      Z0

where A0 is some action, say φ4 action above. We are interested in low-momentum
behaviour of the associated proper vertices, with the momenta conjugated to x1 , ..., xn
much smaller then both Λ0 and Λ1 . Therefore in the insertions we can replace

                             φ0 (x1 )...φ0 (xn ) → φ1 (x1 )...φ1 (xn ) .

Then the functional integral for this correlation function becomes
                 1
                     Z                             Z
                        D[φ1 ] φ1 (x1 )...φ1 (xn )   D[φ̃] e−A0 [φ1 +φ̃] .
                 Z0
Now, the idea is to integrate over the fast part φ̃ first. This operation defines the
new ”effective action” A1 [φ1 ] for the slow part,
                       Z
                           D[φ̃] e−A0 [φ1 +φ̃] = Const e−A1 [φ1 ] .

The constant factor here is not important, because after the same operation it
appears in Z0 as well. Thus, we have

        f.t.⟨ φ0 (x1 )...φ0 (xn ) ⟩ A0 ,Λ0 = f.t.⟨ φ1 (x1 )...φ1 (xn ) ⟩ A1 ,Λ1 ,   pi << Λ .

In other words, if only the low-momentum correlation functions are of concern, the
theory with cutoff Λ0 and action A0 is equivalent to the theory with lower cutoff Λ1
and new action A1 ,
                               A0 , Λ0 → A1 , Λ1 .
Note that I am not writing ↔ here, because this operation can not be undone.
Information about the short-distance interactions, of the scales between Λ−1 0  and
  −1
Λ1 is lost.
    The new action A1 is defined through the functional integral over the fast part
φ̃. Although at this level of discussion precise form of A0 is not important, to get
some idea what this operation amounts to, let us assume for the moment that A0
is the φ4 action. Substituting
                                     φ0 = φ1 + φ̃

                                                 168
we find
                             A0 [φ0 ] = A0 [φ1 ] + Ã[φ̃, φ1 ] ,
and after the integration over φ̃
                               A1 [φ1 ] = A0 [φ1 ] + A′ [φ1 ] ,
where                                                  Z
                                       −A′ [φ1 ]
                          Const e                  =       [Dφ̃] e−Ã[φ̃,φ1 ] .

For A0 of the φ4 form
                            m20 2 λ0
                                                                         
                     1
            Z
                d        2                 3         2 2           3    4
       Ã =    d x (∂ φ̃) +    φ̃ +    (4 φ1 φ̃ + 6 φ1 φ̃ + 4 φ1 φ̃ + φ̃ )
                     2      2       4!
The terms ∂φ1 ∂ φ̃ and φ1 φ̃ do not appear because by definition φ1 and φ̃ carry
different momenta.
    The integral over φ̃ is complicated non-gaussian integral. The functional Ã[φ̃, φ1 ]
in this context should be regarded as the action for φ̃, with φ1 determining the
”source terms” - linear, quadratic and cubic in φ̃.
    As usual, the functional integral over φ̃ can be represented as a sum of the
Feynmann diagrams. Let us denote the φ̃-propagator by a solid line,

                                             k                          Θ(Λ0 > |k| > Λ1 )
           ⟨ φ̃φ̃ ⟩   →                                            =                      ,
                                                                            k 2 + m20

with obvious meaning of the step-function Θ. The remaining terms in Ã give rise

                          λ0                                                          λ0
                                Z                                                             Z
                      = −              φ31 (x) ,                                  = −                 φ21 (x) ,
                          3!       x                                                  2           x




                               Z                                                          Z
                      = −λ0         φ1 (x) ,                                      = −λ0
                                x                                                             x




                                                   169
Here the dotted line represents the field φ1 , the number of the dotted lines showing
the power of φ1 . Each dotted line carry momentum with |k| < Λ1 .
   We have to integrate over φ̃, with φ1 fixed. Therefore all the solid lines in the
diagrams are contracted, while all the dotted lines are left as external legs.
   As usual, the sum of all the diagrams exponentiate in terms of the sum of con-
nected diagrams, i.e. the functional A′ [φ1 ] is given by the sum of all connected
diagrams,
                    A′ [φ1 ] = − sum of all connected diagrams
                                                                 

Also, the diagrams with no external legs can be ignored, because they only contribute
to the constant factor in exp(−A′ ).
    It is obvious that the new action A1 = A0 + A′ does not have simple form of the
φ action. The contributions to A′ contain all powers of φ1 , from diagrams like this
  4




                                                                 ∼ φ6




Moreover, the action A1 is not even formally local. For example, the diagram

                      λ2
                             Z
                     − 0         dd x dd x′ φ21 (x) D̃2 (x − x′ ) φ21 (x′ ) ,
                       8
where D̃ is the φ̃ propagator,
                                                        eikx
                                         Z

                                                               .
                                          Λ0 >|k|>Λ1 k + m0


                                              170
    This non-locality, albeit unpleasant, is not disastrous. Recall that we have al-
ready violated the locality by introducing the cutoff. It is important however that
the additional nonlocality in A1 is of short-range nature. More precise statement
is that the new non-local terms generated this in way admit convergent derivative
expansion.
    This means the following. Suppose we have some diagram evaluated in the
momentum space,

                                     p1
                           pn
                                             p2
                                                        = D(p1 , ..., pn ) .
                                diagram
                                                  p3



One can expand D(p1 , ..., pn ) in a simultaneous power series in all the external
momenta pi , and the series will converge all the way up to the momenta |pi | ≤ Λ1 .
This convergence is the consequence of the fact that all the integration momenta in
the diagram lay above Λ1 . Note that should the integration involve all momenta, as
in usual Feynmann diagrams, similar expansion would converge only up to |pi | < m0 ,
at the best.
    This convergence of the expansions of D(p1 , ..., pn ) implies that the new action
A1 admits meaningful expansion in terms of local composites,
                          ∞
                         X               ∞                                      
                            u2k 2k           v2k 2k
                Z                        X
                     d                                           2
   A1 [φ1 ] =       d x         φ1 (x) +         φ1 (x) (∂φ1 (x)) + higher terms
                        k=1
                            2k!          k=0
                                             2k!

where the ”higher terms” involve both the higher powers of ∂φ1 , and the terms with
the higher derivatives of φ1 . The most significant difference from truly local action
is that these higher terms include derivatives of arbitrarily high order. However it
is assumed that the full expansion converges for the fields φ1 , in which only the
Fourier modes with |k| < Λ1 are present. I will call this form ”quasi-local”.
    Our conclusion at this point is that by integrating out the fast part φ̃, the
theory described originally by the action A0 , with the cutoff Λ0 , can be equivalently
described by new action A1 , with the lower cutoff Λ1 ,

                                   A0 , Λ0    →        A1 , Λ1 .


                                              171
The new action however has complicated quasi-local form.
   At first glance it may look as a step in wrong direction. But it becomes very
powerful move when combined with another step, which is to make a variable trans-
formation
                            φ1 (x) = Z −1/2 (L) φ′0 (x/L)
in the functional integral
                           1
                                Z
                                    [Dφ1 ] φ1 (x1 )...φ1 (xn ) e−A1 [φ1 ] ,
                           Z1
where L is the ratio
                                        L = Λ0 /Λ1 > 1 ,
and Z 1/2 (L) is a factor to be discussed later. This is the dilation with the factor
1/L, which returns the cutoff from Λ1 back to the original value Λ0 . We have
            Z                  Z                            Z
                          ikx               i(Lk)(x/L)   −d
  φ1 (x) =          ϕ(k) e =          ϕ(k) e           =L           ϕ(q/L) eiq (x/L) ,
             |k|<Λ1                  |k|<Λ1                               |q|<Λ0

so that                                                Z
                        φ′0 (x) = L−d Z −1/2 (L)                  ϕ(k/L) eikx
                                                        |k|<Λ0

indeed contains all the wave-vectors with |k| < Λ0 .
   For the action this amounts to transformation

                                        A1 [φ1 ] = A′0 [φ′0 ] ,

where A′0 is obtained from A1 simply by re-scaling of the coordinates, plus the field
renormalization. We then have for correlation functions

          ⟨ φ0 (x1 )...φ0 (xn ) ⟩ A0 ,Λ0 = Z −n/2 (L) ⟨ φ0 (x1 /L)...φ0 (xn /L) ⟩ A′ ,Λ0 ,
                                                                                    0


i.e. the theories described by A0 and A′0 , with the same cutoff Λ0 are different only
by the scale transformation, with the scale factor L. The transformation

                                    RG(L) :          A0 → A′0

is the Wilson’s RG transformation. It represents the scale transformation in the
space of effective actions.


                                                 172
17      More on Wilson’s RG
The last time we have defined the Wilson’s Renormalization Group Transformation.
This transformation acts on the action functionals A[φ0 ],

                                              A → A′ ,

which represent cutoff field theories, i.e. field theories equipped with some cutoff
regularization, with cutoff parameter Λ. The transformation depends on a parameter
L ≥ 1 and a function Z(L), and consists of two steps.

   STEP 1: Integrating out the “fast” degrees of freedom.
   The field φ0 (x) is split into its “slow” and “fast” parts,

                                    φ0 (x) = φ1 (x) + φ̃(x) ,

where
                     Z                                           Z
                                        ikx
          φ1 (x) =             ϕ(k) e         ,        φ̃(x) =               ϕ(k) eikx .   (17.1)
                     |k|<Λ/L                                     Λ/L<|k|<Λ

Integrating the part φ̃ out one defines new action
                                           Z
                                −A1 [φ1 ]
                        Const e           = [Dφ̃] e−A[φ1 +φ̃]                              (17.2)

which depends only on the “slow” degrees of freedom, with the cutoff parameter
Λ/L.

   STEP 2: Rescaling of the coordinates x → x/L.
   One makes the field variable transformation
                                                  1
                               φ1 (x) = Z − 2 (L) φ0 (x/L)                                 (17.3)

in the functional integral over φ1 , defining new integration variable φ0 . This trans-
formation returns the cutoff parameter to its original value Λ, and yields new action
                                                       1
                          A′ [φ0 (x)] = A1 [Z 2 (L) φ1 (xL)] .                             (17.4)

                                1
   In principle, the factor Z 2 (L) in (17.3),(17.4) is arbitrary. Field renormalizations
        1
φ0 → Z 2 φ0 do not change physical content of field theory, it can be thought of as

                                                      173
some sort of gauge transformation 13 . When we characterize field theories by their
action functionals, two actions different only in such renormalization are equivalent,
                                             1
                                       A[Z 2 φ0 ] ∼ A[φ0 ] .

In practice this redundancy is usualy eliminated by some field normalization condi-
                                                                           1
tion which plays role of gauge fixing condition. In this case the factor Z 2 (L) must
be adjusted so that the transformation A → A′ does preserve that condition.

  It follows from the above definition that if A′ is obtained from A by the Wilson’s
RG transformation, the corresponding correlation functions are related as,
                                                         n
             f.t. ⟨φ0 (x1 ) · · · φ0 (xn )⟩A′ = f.t. Z 2 (L) ⟨φ0 (x1 L) · · · φ0 (xn L)⟩A

where it is understood that the furier transforms of the correlation functions are
taken and the comparison is made at low values of the momenta |pi | << Λ. In
other words, the theory described by the action A′ differs from that described by A
by a scale transformation x → x/L only. The Wilson’s RG shows what it takes to
make such scale transformation in field theory. Just rescaling the coordinates in the
action, as is done in (17.3), is not sufficient. The “naive” scaling transformation,
represented by the step 2 above, must be supplemented by the integration over the
fast modes in the step 1. Roughly speaking, the dilation x → x/L increases the
spatial density of the field degrees of freedom, and one has to “dilute” them back to
the original density by performing the step 1. In this sense the step 1 can be thought
of as representing the transformation of the measure in the functional integral.

   One remark is in order here. In general, implementation of the step 1 of the
Wilson’s RG - elimination of the fast modes - depends on particular form of the cutoff
regularization implied. The definition (17.1) corresponds to the “sharp momentum
cutoff”. We know that other cutoff regularizations are possible. For example, in
the lattice regularization, where the field degrees of freedom are associated with
the lattice cells, the procedure described above is hardly natural. Instead, one can
  13
     Much more general class of “gauge transformations” can be applied to field theories, the so
called “field redefinitions”. One considers generic local change of variables of the form

                                  φ0 (x) → f (φ0 (x), ∂µ φ0 (x), · · · ) ,

which leaves the quasilocal nature of the action (16.5) intact. Here we will not consider implications
of this huge ambiguity. Of course, it is not to be mixed with the usual gauge transformations in
gauge field theories.


                                                   174
consider a sublattice whose cells consist of Ld elementary cells (L must be integer
in this case). The “slow” field φ1 is then defined as the original field averaged over
these greater cells, and the step 1 amounts to integrating over φ0 with φ1 fixed. I
am mentioning this here to stress that the two step procedure of the Wilson’s RG
can be considered as a general idea rather then an exact prescription, the idea whose
implementation depends on the problem in hand. However, in this discussion we
will follow the particular implementation described in (17.1).

    We have seen that even if the original action A has some simple form, say φ4 ,
the result of the Wilson’s RG transformation is usually a complicated “quasilocal”
action which contains all possible terms with arbitrarily high powers of the field and
with arbitrarily high powers and orders of the derivatives. On the other hand, if A
is generic quasilocal action, one can expect that the action A′ will again be of the
quasilocal form. Under this assumption, the Wilson’s RG transformation acts in the
space of all quasilocal action functionals. In what follows I will use the notation Σ
for this infinite-dimensional space, so that

                                   RG : Σ → Σ .

Also, I will denote the RG transformation as

                                   A′ = RGl { A } ,

using more convenient parameter

                                     l = log L ≥ 0

instead of L. It is not difficult to understand that this transformations obeys the
composition law

             RGl+l′ { A } = RGl { RGl′ { A } } ,      RG0 { A } = A .            (17.5)

These properties define one-parameter semigroup. It is not a group because a group
contains inverse elements, and inverse to RGl in general cannot be defined. The
reason is simple but important - the step 1 of the RG transformation leads to a
loss of information about the interactions at the cutoff scales; this step cannot be
“undone”.
    In general exact evaluation of the RG transformation for arbitrary l is at least
as difficult as exact evaluation of the full functional integral, i.e. usually hopeless.
If the interaction is not small no reliable approximations are even avalable. One

                                          175
can hope however to find good approximations for RGl with l small enough. Then
according to the composition law (17.5) the transformation RGN l can be obtained
as N th iteration of RGl . While it is not clear how to evaluate the functional integral
at ones, may be it is possible to do it step by step, integrating out the shortest scale
degrees of freedom, and then repeating this again and again.
   It is usualy most convenient to consider the infinitesimal RG transformation

                        RGδl { A } = A + B{ A } δl + O(δl2 ) ,

where
                                         d
                              B{ A } =      RGl { A }     .
                                         dl           l=0

It follows from (17.5) that the one-parameter family of actions

                                   Al = RGl { A0 } ,

where A0 is an arbitrary “initial” action, satisfies the differential equation
                                 d
                                    Al = B{ Al } .                                 (17.6)
                                 dl
which is usually referred to as Wilson’s RG flow equation. It generates the scale
transformations in the space of quasilocal actions Σ, namely, two actions Al1 and
Al2 belonging to the same integral curve Al (sometimes called the RG trajectory)
describe the theories which differ by the scale transform x → x exp(l1 − l2 ). Thus
the problem splits into two parts: (i) Evaluation of the RG operator B{A} and (ii)
Integration the RG flow equation (17.6).

   The RG flow equation (17.6) is written above in very abstract form. To make it
substantial one has to introduce some coordinates on Σ. Generic quasilocal action
can be written as (I assume φ0 → −φ0 symmetry)
                       X∞                  ∞
                             u2k 2k            v2k 2k
               Z                           X
                    d
      A[φ0 ] =    d x             φ0 (x) +         φ0 (∂φ0 )2 (x) +
                        k=1
                             2k!           k=0
                                               2k!
                           ∞                                     
                          X    w2k+1 2k+1        2 2
                                      φ0 (∂φ0 ) ∂ φ0 (x) + · · · .         (17.7)
                          k=0
                                 2k!

The coefficients (“coupling constants”) u2k , v2k , w2k+1 , · · · can be taken as such co-
ordinates. In general there are infinitely many couplings and therefore the space Σ

                                           176
is infinite-dimensional. One of these parameters can be eliminated by appropriate
renormalization of φ0 , for instance one can use this freedom to set

                                              v0 = 1

(the “gauge fixing” discussed above).
   In terms of these coordinates the RG flow equation (17.6) can be written as

                      d
                        u2k (l) = Bu2k ({u2k (l)}, {v2k (l)}, {w2k+1 (l)}, · · · ) ;
                     dl
                      d
                        v2k (l) = Bv2k ({u2k (l)}, {v2k (l)}, {w2k+1 (l)}, · · · ) ;
                     dl
                     etc                                                                    (17.8)

where u2k (l), v2k (l), · · · are the coupling constants corresponding to the action Al ,
and B’s are some functions of all these infinitely many coordinates. Of course it is
impossible to calculate exactly all these functions. Neither can one hope to integrate
exactly all the RG flow equations. In practical calculations some approximations are
necessary. Most importantly one must find some finite-dimensional approximation
to the infinite-dimensional space Σ. But before doing any calculations it is useful to
think about what are the properties of the RG flow we must be interested in.

Topological properties of RG flow14
Some general topological properties of RG flow can be discussed in an abstract form
without reference to specific form of the RG transformation. Some general assump-
tions will be made concerning the actions generated by the RG transformations.
Most importantly, we will make an assumption already mentioned above, namely,
that the RG transformation RGl applied to a quasilocal action A produces an action
Al which is quasilocal. In other words, our main assumption is that RG : Σ → Σ.
This is not a trivial assumption. I remind you that our term “quasilocal action”
means not only an action which admits formal derivative expansion (17.7), but also
that this expansion converges for all fields φ0 which contain only fourier components
         <
with |k| ∼ Λ. Roughly speaking quasilocal action can contain nonlocal interactions
of the range ∼ Λ−1 but not much larger. There is no proof to this statement, but it
holds for many known implementations of the RG procedure, so we accept it.
  14
    The discussion in this section follows closely that in K. Wilson, G. Kogut, ”The RG and epsilon
expansion”, Phys.Rep. 12C (1974)



                                                177
    Under this assumption, if A0 ∈ Σ, the associated RG trajectory Al , i.e. the
solution of the flow equation (17.6) with the initial condition Al=0 = A0 , lies inside
Σ for all l > 0.


                                                     Al

                               A0                            Σ




    The trajectory Al , as a function of the parameter l, gives a set of actions which
all describe essentially the same physical system, the only difference being in the
coordinate rescalings. Namely one must make an l dependent change of scale when
relating Al to physics.
    It is useful to discuss in terms of physical mass m, or, as it is more common in
statistical mechanics, in terms of the “correlation length” Rc = m−1 . This is defined
as follows. Consider the two-point correlation function. Its typical large-distance
behavior in Euclidean space is (recall for instance your solution of the behavior of
the Klein-Gordon propagator D(x) at |x| → ∞)
                                             |x|
                        ⟨φ0 (x)φ0 (0)⟩ ≃ e− Rc     as |x| → ∞ .

Let Rc be the correlation length associated with our system described by the action
A0 , with the cutoff Λ. Of course Al describes the same system, but in the units in
which the cutoff associated with Al is still Λ, the correlation length will be

                                    Rc (l) = e− l Rc .

   One can define various subspaces, or “surfaces”, in Σ. The elements of Σ can
be separated according to their correlation length. The following picture shows
schematically the surfaces of constant Rc .

                                            R c = 10 Λ-1
                                                   R c = 100 Λ-1

                                              R c = ΟΟ




                                          178
    Of special interest is the “critical surface” with Rc = ∞, or m = 0. We denote
it Σc .
    Important subspace is the subspace Σ(l) generated by applying RGl to Σ itself,

                                      Σ(l) = RGl {Σ} .


                                                        Σ(l)
                                     Σ




                                                                 Σ ( l + l’ )




                       Σ ( οο )


       As we have assumed that RGl acts in Σ,

                                          Σ(l) ⊂ Σ .

Obviously the subspaces Σ(l) are nested, i.e.

                            Σ(l + l′ ) ⊂ Σ(l) for any l, l′ > 0

(this follows from Σ(l + l′ ) = RGl {Σ(l′ )}).
    Strictly speaking the space Σ is infinite-dimensional. However we assume that
in many important respects it can be treated as finite dimensional one. This is
equivalent to the assumption that it is possible to build a system of approximations
to the RG transformation in which Σ is replaced by a spaces of large but finite
dimensionalities, and that such approximations converge to exact RG transformation
when this dimensionality goes to infinity15 . If dimensionality of Σ is understood
in this sense, the spaces Σ(l) for finite l have the same dimensionality as Σ itself.
However, the space Σ(∞), obtained as the limit l → ∞ of Σ(l), can be much smaller.
The basic idea of the Wilson’s RG approach is in the hope that Σ(∞) has many less
  15
   Of course these statements are formulated in a way which is less then satisfactory from math-
ematical point of view. Mathematical rigor can be achieved only in some simplified models of the
RG transformation.


                                              179
dimensions then Σ. The reason why Σ(∞) is expected to be much smaller then Σ
is the following: For large l the action Al describes the physical system with actual
interactions of much shorter range then the range of interactions which appear in
the action Al itself. Remember, Al is physically equivalent to A0 , with the scales
changed by the factor e− l . It is unlikely that arbitrary action in Σ has this property.
Thus Σ(∞) is expected to be only a small subset in Σ.
    The differential equation (17.6), i.e.
                                         d
                                            A = B{A}
                                         dl
can be integrated backward in l as well as forward. Suppose A is in Σ but not in
Σ(l). Then A− l , obtained by integrating backwards, can not lie in Σ 16 .



                                                         A
                               Σ          Σ( l )




    The reason A− l is not in Σ is likely to be one of the following: A− l can fail
to be quasilocal, i.e. A− l may contain long-range interactions. Or A− l can have
other pathological properties, like some coefficients which control convergence of the
functional integral can become such that the functional integral with A− l no longer
exists (like λ in φ4 theory can become negative), so that the action A− l does not
define meaningful theory. Anyhow, for large l the actions A− l are typically outside
Σ.
   Important exceptions from this general rule are the actions belonging to the
subspace Σ(∞). If one takes
                                 A ∈ Σ(∞) ,
and obtains A− l by integrating backwards,
                                         A− l ∈ Σ(∞) .
This is true for if A− l is in Σ but not in Σ(∞) we have
                                       RGl {A− l } = A ,
 16
      If A−l ∈ Σ then A = RGl {A−l } ∈ Σ(l).


                                                   180
so that A can be reached in finite “time” l from some point in Σ, but this contradicts
the definition of Σ(∞). In short, starting in Σ(∞) one can integrate back and forth,
still remaining within Σ(∞).
    For this reason the subspace Σ(∞) is of central importance in local Quantum
Field Theory. The problem of QFT is to obtain finite local theory in the limit
Λ → ∞, with appropriately adjusted “bare action” A(Λ). “Integarting backward”
is essentially considering the actions which lead to the same physics as the action
A0 one starts with, but equipped with larger and larger cutoff Λ, mesured in the
units of m. It is then clear that finding renormalized local theories is equivalent to
finding actions A from which one can “integrate backward” without limits. For this
reason it is natural to identify Σ(∞) with the space of local QFT. We will come
back to this point many times over.




                                         181
18      Fixed Points of RG
At generic point in Σ the pattern of the RG flow is rather boring - it just flows.
Something interesting is expected to happen near singular points of the flow. Of
these the simplest and probably the most important ones are so called Fixed Points.
   A fixed point is an action A∗ which satisfy the equation

                                     B{ A∗ } = 0 .

Associated trajectory is trivial,

                                Al = A∗    for all l .

As the correlation length measured in the units Λ is

                                    Rc (l) = e− l Rc

for Al , this means that for A∗ we must have

                                    either Rc = 0

                                    or    Rc = ∞ .
By the definition, A∗ with Rc = ∞ belongs to the “critical surface”,

                               A∗ ∈ Σc    if    Rc = ∞ .

We call the fixed points A∗ with Rc = ∞ the “critical” fixed points.

    To illustrate the topological ideas related to the Wilson’s RG, I will describe the
simplest possible form of topology. There are many ways in which topology of the
RG flow can be more complicated but complications can be understood ones the
simplest picture is grasped. In this analysis I will assume continuity of the RG flow,
i.e. the functions B in (17.8) are continuous functions of their arguments. Again,
general proof of this statement is not available, but various examples support this
assumption.
   Assume there are only two fixed points, one with Rc = 0 and one with Rc = ∞.
Denote them P0 and P∞ .




                                          182
                                             P0
                                                             Rc = 0

                                                              R c = Λ-1



                                      P oo               R c = ΟΟ



    As Rc (l) = e− l Rc , any trajectory which starts at finite Rc will approach Rc = 0
and then P0 . On the other hand trajectories which start on Σc will approach P∞ .
The only point where some trajectory can leave Σc is P∞ 17 . So there must be a
trajectory (let’s call it U ) going from P∞ to P0 .


                                                    P0



                                       U
                                                  G

                                             P oo             Σc

   Consider also a trajectory G which starts close to Σc . This one first approaches
P∞ while staying close to Σc , then spends very long “time” l in the vicinity of P∞
(by continuity B{A} is small when A is close to P∞ ) and then departs along some
way close to U (recall that U is the only trajectory that leaves Σc ), finally going
towards P0 .
    Now let us look for the space Σ(∞) in this picture. It is clear that Σ(∞) is
U , with the points P∞ and P0 included. Starting with any action A on the curve
U one can back along the trajectory arbitrary amount of “time” l without leaving
the space Σ (actually, without leaving U ). The reason is that Rc = ∞ on U only
  17
   Suppose a trajectory ”leaves” Σc at some Pc ∈ Σc . Obviously Pc = liml=∞ RG−l {P } for any
P on such trajectory.



                                              183
at the point P∞ . To reach P∞ from any point on U with finite Rc takes infinite
“time” l (when moving backwards). Starting from other point in Σ and integrating
backwards, one generally leaves Σ in sufficient amount of “time”.
    We see from this example the role played by critical fixed points in the formation
of the space Σ(∞). Therefore finding critical fixed points is of central importance
in the RG approach.

Critical Fixed Points.
Suppose A∗ is a critical fixed point, i.e.

                                     B{ A∗ } = 0 .

and associated Rc = ∞. Consider vicinity of A∗ . Let

                                    A = A∗ + δA ,

where δA is infinitesimal. The variation δA must be thought of as a vector in the
tangent space T Σ taken at the point A∗ ,

                                     δA ∈ T Σ|A∗ .


                                              δA
                           TΣ|
                             | A*
                                                              Σ




   One can linearize the RG transformation near A∗ ,

                         B{A∗ + δA} = K{δA} + O(δA2 ) ,

where K is a linear operator acting in this tangent space,

                               K:     T Σ|A∗ → T Σ|A∗ .

As it is conventional in the theory of linear operators, I will write

                                 K{δA} ≡ K δA .                                (18.1)

                                             184
The RG flow in this close vicinity of A∗ is then described by linear differential
equation
                                d
                                  (δA) = K δA .
                               dl
   This equation is easily solved provided one can find eigenvectors Ψα and associ-
ated eigenvalues κα of the operator K,

                                   K Ψα = κα Ψα .

The solution takes the form
                                      X
                            δA(l) =        Cα eκα l Ψα ,                         (18.2)
                                       α

where the constants Cα are determined by the initial vector δA(0) = δA0 .
    It is easy to understand that the pattern of the RG flow in the vicinity of A∗ is
directly related to the eigenvalues κα . To illustrate this let us assume that there are
only two eigenvalues (this correspond to a two-dimensional approximation to Σ),
both real, one positive and one negative,

                                       κ+ > 0 ,

                                       κ− < 0 .
The picture below shows the pattern of corresponding RG flow in the vicinity of
the fixed point. There is a natural coordinate system on T Σ|A∗ associated with the
eigenvectors Ψ+ and Ψ− .

                                                    Ψ+

                                           U
                                                   G
                                                                Ψ-


                                                           Σc

   All trajectories starting at the Ψ− axis never leave it; they flow towards the fixed
point A∗ . As the correlation length can only decrease with l, the entire axis Ψ− must

                                           185
belong to Σc . The trajectories starting at the Ψ+ also remain at this axis, but they
flow away from the fixed point. For sufficiently large l these trajectories leave the
domain of validity of the linear approximation (18.1), and (18.2) says nothing about
their ultimate destination. The same is true for any generic trajectory G starting
at neither axis: at sufficiently large l it leaves the vicinity of the fixed point and
its fate cannot be predicted within the linear approximation. However there is an
important difference between the generic trajectories like G and any trajectory U
that lies on the axis Ψ+ . Starting at any point on the axis Ψ+ one can “integrate
backward” for indefinite negative l without leaving the vicinity of the fixed point
A∗ ; in fact corresponding A− l approaches A∗ as l → ∞. This is in contrast with
the negative l behavior of generic trajectories; associated A− l leave the vicinity
of A∗ for sufficiently large l, and the question if they remain in Σ for l → ∞
cannot be answered within the linear approximation. On the other hand the linear
approximation is good enough to recognize in this example at least one-dimensional
component of the subspace Σ(∞) associated with the fixed point A∗ ; the axis Ψ+
is the linear approximation to this component. Precise statement is
                                 Ψ+ ⊂ T Σ(∞)|A∗ .


    It is not difficult to generalize the conclusions obtained in the example above.
It is useful to define two special subspaces of the tangent space T Σ|A∗ . Let F+ be
the linear envelope of the eigenvectors of K corresponding to positive eigenvalues κ;
with some abuse of notations we can write
                                 F+ = ⊕α:κα >0 Ψα .
Similarly, let F− be the sum of eigenspaces corresponding to negative κα , i.e.
                                 F− = ⊕α:κα <0 Ψα .
This separation leaves unaccounted very interesting possibility of having zero eigen-
values, later we will discuss it separately. Also, it is assumed that all eigenvalues
of K are real. At this point this assumption looks arbitrary. We will see later that
having complex eigenvalues contradicts quantum mechanical interpretation of field
theory.
   Simple analysis of (18.2) shows that

   • The fixed point A∗ belongs to a critical surface Σc whose dimensionality coin-
cides with the dimensionality of F− , and
                                   F− = T Σc |A∗ .

                                         186
    • The fixed point A∗ also belongs to some component of Σ(∞); the dimension-
ality of this component is equal or greater then the dimensionality of F+ , and

                                 F+ ⊂ T Σ(∞) |A∗ .


    Let us define the subspace U (A∗ ) ⊂ Σ(∞) by the condition that all points of
U (A∗ ) lie on RG trajectories which, when “integrated backward”, converge to the
fixed point A∗ , i.e.

                       A0 ∈ U (A∗ ) ⇒ Al → A∗ as l → −∞

The subspace U (A∗ ) is called the unstable manifold of the fixed point A∗ . Obviously
A∗ ∈ U (A∗ ). If we ignore the possibility of zero eigenvalues then the tangent space
to U (A∗ ) taken at the fixed point A∗ itself coincides with the space F+ , i.e.

                                 F+ = T U (A∗ )|A∗ .

   The reason why Σ(∞) can have dimensionality greater then then that of the
unstable manifold U (A∗ ) is illustrated by the following simple example. Suppose we
have two fixed points, the A∗ and another one, A′∗ , such that A∗ ∈ U (A′∗ ).

                                  U( A )
                                      *




                                       A
                                           *

                                                               A’
                                                                 *


    Then there must be at least one RG trajectory in U (A′∗ ) which flows to A∗
(this is by the definition of unstable manifold U ). This trajectory has Rc = ∞ and
therefore it simultaneously belongs to Σ(∞) and Σc . It follows that the unstable
manifold U (A∗ ) has at least one dimension less then U (A′∗ ).

    Understanding the global topological properties of the RG flow, like the one illus-
trated in the above example, are beyond the capabilities of linear analysis. However,
as we have seen, it is within the power of linear analysis to prove that each fixed

                                           187
point A∗ generates a manifold U (A∗ ) which belongs to Σ(∞). The dimensionality n
of this manifold equals to the number of positive eigenvalues of the operator K. In
other words, the fixed point A∗ generates n-parameter family of finite local QFT.

   The above discussion was somewhat abstract. It is useful to understand the
meaning of the abstract notions of the above linear analysis, like tangent spaces, in
terms of local field degrees of freedom.
   Recall that generic quasilocal action A can be written as integral

stressing the fact that RG transformation acts on the fields linearly; additional index

   Let us assume now that A is the fixed-point action,

                                             A = A∗ .

                                                 190
Then A′ coincides with A, and (18.5) yields linear relation for the correlation func-
tions of the composite fields. Recall that by the composition law of the RG trans-
formations,
                            RGl+l′ {∗} = RGl {RGl′ {∗}} ,
in this case the coefficients z must obey the equation
                                              X
                              zαβ (l + l′ ) =   zαγ (l′ ) zγβ (l) ,
                                                 γ

where I changed the notations z(l) = z(L = el ). In other words these coefficients, as
the functions of l, must satisfy linear differential equation with constant coefficients,
                                  d β           X
                                     zα (l) = −   Dαγ zγβ (l) ,
                                  dl            γ

where
                                         d β
                                      Dαβ = −
                                           z (l)|l=0 .
                                         dl α
The coefficients Dαβ are matrix elements of of the linear operator D corresponding
to the infinitesimal transformation (18.5)

                                RGδl,A∗ = I − δl D + O(δl2 ) .

   Let us denote Φα (x) the eigenvectors of the operator D, i.e. special linear com-
binations of the composite fields Oα (x) which satisfy the equations

                                   D Φα ≡ Dαβ Φβ = Dα Φα ,

where I use the notation Dα for the corresponding eigenvalues of D. Then

                                    RGl,A∗ Φα = e−l Dα Φα ,

and for the fixed-point theory the equation (18.5) reads

   ⟨Φα1 (x1 ) · · · Φαn (xn )⟩|A∗ = L−Dα1 · · · L−Dα1 ⟨Φα1 (x1 /L) · · · Φαn (xn /L)⟩|A∗ , (18.6)

i.e. the above correlation functions are homogeneous functions of the coordinates
x1 , . . . , xn , of the degree Dα1 + · · · + Dαn . For the two-point correlation functions,
which depend only on the distance between the two points, it follows from (18.6)
                                                          Gα,β
                             ⟨Φα (x)Φβ (x′ )⟩|A∗ =                    ,
                                                      |x − x′ |Dα +Dβ

                                                191
where Gα,β are constants. In general, the equation expresses the scale invariance of
the theory described by the fixed-point action A∗ . The eigenvalues Dα are called
the scale dimensions (or anomalous scale dimensions) of the fields Φα . If A∗ is not
a free-field theory, there is no reason to expect them to coincide with “canonical
dimensions”. I’d like to stress that these anomalous dimensions as well as the basic
“scaling” fields Φα are defined relative to given fixed point A∗ . We will see that in
the theory of continuous phase transitions these dimensions determine the critical
exponents.

   We have seen that the space F is identical to the tangent space T ΣA∗ . and
therefore the above operator D must be closely related to the operator K which is
the linearization of the RG generator B{A} near the fixed point,

                                B{A∗ + δA} = K δA .                             (18.7)

The relation is very simple. Any eigenvector Ψα of K,

                                     K Ψα = κα Ψα ,

is the integral                           Z
                                   Ψα =       dd x Φα (x) .

Therefore the eigenvalues are related as

                                      κα = d − Dα ,

where d is the number of the space dimensions. TheR term d appears because in
the step 2 of the RG transformation of the integral dd x Φ(x) we have to make
additional transformation of the integration measure dd x,
                         Z                    Z
                             d              d
                            d x Φ(x/L) = L      dd xΦ(x) .


   As we have seen, the roles played by positive and negative eigenvalues of K in
the formation of the RG flow near A∗ are very different. The negative-eigenvalue
subspace F− corresponds to the critical surface, whereas the positive-eigenvalue
subspace F+ is the linear approximation to the unstable manifold U (A∗ ) ⊂ Σ(∞).
The common terminology is as follows. One refers to

         Φα       with Dα > d (i.e. with κα < 0) as the “irrelevant′′ field ,

                                              192
         Φα    with Dα = d (i.e. with κα = 0) as the “marginal′′ field ,
          Φα   with Dα < d (i.e. with κα > 0) as the “relevant′′ field .
If marginal fields are absent, the dimensionality of U (A∗ ) equals the number of
relevant fields.
   The situation is more complex if the marginal fields are present. Let me assume
that there is only one marginal field ΦM . By definition, the relevant (irrelevant) fields
correspond to deformations δA of the fixed-point action which increase (decrease)
with the RG flow in the linear approximation in δA. The deformation
                                           Z
                              δAM = λM        dd x ΦM (x)

corresponding to the marginal field does not change in the linear approximation,
but it may well increase or decrease with the RG flow if we go beyond the linear
approximation, say take into account the terms ∼ δA2M . If δAM increases with
the RG flow for λM small enough, the deformation δAM (and the field ΦM ) is
called marginally relevant; similarly, if it decreases, one refers to ΦM as marginally
irrelevant field.
    In many important respects the marginally relevant (marginally irrelevant) field
can be treated on the same footing with the relevant (irrelevant) ones. Thus, if a
marginally relevant field is present, the unstable manifold U (A∗ ) is generated by the
subspace F+′ which includes this marginally relevant field along with all the relevant
fields,
                                   F+′ = T U (A∗ )|A∗ .
We are going to have more precise statements after studying some examples.

Gaussian fixed point
Let us consider the simplest fixed point for which all calculations can be performed
explicitly, the so called Gaussian fixed point. It is described by the massless free
action

    The eigenvalues Dn are found immediately, without computing exact form of the
eigenvectors Φn . Indeed, the eq. (18.8) shows that the operator D acts on the fields

                   is the canonical dimension of φ0 in d-dimensional space. The
dimensions Dn coincide with the canonical dimensions of the composite fields φn0 ,

                                           195
which is not surprising because we are considering the free massless field theory
A∗,G .
   Dimensionality of the space F+ (i.e. the space of relevant fields) associated with
the fixed point A∗,G depends on d. The field Φ2 = 12 : φ20 : is always relevant; its
dimension
                                D2 = d − 2 < d .
It remains the only relevant field for all d > 6. For d < 6 the field Φ3 = 3!1 : φ30 :
with the dimension
                                          3
                                   D3 = (d − 2)
                                          2
becomes relevant, too. For d < 4 it is joined by the field Φ4 = 4!1 : φ40 :. The
situation is summarized in the table

                                d                 relevant fields

                               d>6                    φ20
                           6>d>4                       φ20 , φ30
                       4 > d > 10/3                    φ20 , φ30 , φ40
                     10/3 > d > 3                   φ20 , φ30 , φ40 , φ50
                                        ···
                    2n          2(n + 1)
                         >d>                         φ20 , φ30 , · · · , φn0
                   n−2            n−1
At d = 2 all polynomials of φ0 are relevant.

    Note that the above classification coincides with our earlier classification of the
perturbatively renormalizable scalar field theories. We see that in generic dimension-
ality d the perturbatively renormalizable field theories correspond to the unstable
manifold U (A∗,G ) of the gaussian fixed point. Also, we realize now that this classifi-
cation of renormalizable field theories is relevant only to the vicinity of the gaussian
fixed point (after all the perturbative expansion is developed around this point); if
we manage to find more complex fixed point, its own unstable manifold will give
rise to a family of finite local field theories which may be not too similar to the
perturbatively defined renormalizable field theories we considered before.
   Thus we have a correspondence between the unstable manifold U (A∗,G ) associ-
ated with the Gaussian fixed point and perturbatively renormalizable scalar field

                                          196
theories in different dimensions d. This correspondence is more subtle if d takes
special values such that one of the fields Φn acquires the dimension Dn = d, i.e. be-
comes marginal. This happens for instance at d = 6 with Φ3 ∼ φ30 and at d = 4 with
Φ4 ∼ φ40 . The last case is of most interest to us, and we take it as main example.

φ4 flow at d = 4
As at d = 4 the field Φ4 is marginal, we have

                                        200
19      Some Properties of RG Flow
In the previous Section we have discussed the basic properties of the Gaussian fixed
point A∗ G at different dimensions d. At special values of d the fixed point theory
has marginal operators. Thus, at d = 4 the field Φ4 ∼ φ4 is marginal, and as more
careful calculation shows, it is marginally irrelevant. This is expressed by the RG
flow equation (18.16) (note that it is equivalent to the leading term in the beta
function (15.19)).

RG flow and Poincaré-Dulac normal form
It is interesting to see, in general, what is special about the marginal deformations
from the mathematical point of view. Discussing on the abstract level, let us assume
that we have generic critical fixed point A∗ ∈ Σ. Let y α be some local coordinates on
Σ, covering some neighborhood of A∗ and chosen in such a way that the fixed point
corresponds to y α = 0. One can think of y α as the collection of coupling constants
u2k −u2k,∗ , v2k −v2k,∗ , · · · , where u’s, v’s, etc, are the coefficients appearing in writing
A in terms of various monomials in φ0 and its derivatives, and u∗ ’s, etc, are their
fixed-point values. Anyhow, the RG flow equation
                                        d
                                           A = B{A}
                                        dl
can be written in such coordinates as
                                    d α
                                       y = B α ({y}) ,                                   (19.1)
                                    dl
where B α are some functions of all y’s. As y = 0 is assumed to be a fixed point,
these functions satisfy the condition

                                        B α ({0}) = 0 .

Let us assume that these functions can be expanded into Taylor series around the
fixed point,
                                             α
                      B α ({y}) = Kβα y β + Cβγ yβ yγ + · · · ,
where K, C, etc, are some constants, and summation over repeated indices is as-
sumed. The coefficients Kβα are the matrix elements of the operator K, the lin-
earization of the RG generator B near the fixed point,

                             B{A∗ + δA} = K δA + O(δA2 ) .

                                              201
By appropriate linear transformation of the coordinates y: y α → Mβα y β the matrix
Kβα can be brought to diagonal form19 . In these coordinates the flow equation (19.1)
takes the form
                              d α
                                 y = κα y α + cαβγ y β y γ + · · · ,              (19.2)
                              dl
where κα are the eigenvalues of K, and no summation over α is assumed in the first
term in the r.h.s.; the coefficients c are related to C by a linear transformation, in
an obvious way.
   No further simplification of the linear term in the r.h.s. of (19.2) is possible.
However one may ask if the quadratic and higher order terms in the r.h.s. can be
simplified by nonlinear coordinate transformation

                             y α = y α ({ỹ}) = ỹ α + aαβγ ỹ β ỹ γ + · · · .

    Under coordinate transformations the functions B α transform as components
of a vector field on Σ. Simple calculation shows that in terms of ỹ the RG flow
equations take the form
                                d α
                                   ỹ = κα ỹ α + c̃αβγ ỹ β ỹ γ + · · · ,
                                dl
where
                                c̃αβγ = cαβγ − (κα − κβ − κγ ) aαβγ .
One can completely eliminate the quadratic term by taking
                                                    cαβγ
                                       aαβγ =                ;
                                                κα − κβ − κγ
with this choice
                              d α
                                 ỹ = κα ỹ α + O(ỹ 3 ) .
                              dl
This is true provided the combinations of the eigenvalues appearing in the denomi-
nators do not vanish. However if some triplet of the eigenvalues satisfy the resonance
condition
                                  κα − κβ − κγ = 0
the “resonance” term
                                                cαβγ y β y γ
 19
      Non-diagonalizable forms contradict unitarity (positivity of norms)


                                                   202
(no summation) cannot be eliminated.
    Similar situation takes place in the higher orders. If components B α of a vector
field B can be written as power series

             B α ({y}) = κα y α + cαβ1 β2 y β1 y β2 + cαβ1 β2 β3 y β1 y β2 y β3 + · · · .   (19.3)

in some local coordinates y α , then by means appropriate analytic coordinate trans-
formation

                   y α → y α + aαβ1 β2 y β1 y β2 + aαβ1 β2 β3 y β1 y β2 y β3 + · · ·        (19.4)

one can exterminate all nonlinear terms in (19.3), except for the so-called resonance
terms. A term

                                    cαβ1 β2 ···βn y β1 y β2 · · · y βn                      (19.5)

in the function B α is called the resonance term if the following resonance condition

                                 κα = κβ1 + κβ2 + · · · + κβn                               (19.6)

is satisfied. One can always adjust the coefficients a in (19.4), order by order in y,
in such a way that in new coordinates
                            d α
                               y = κα y α + Resonance Terms                                 (19.7)
                            dl
If there are no resonances, all nonlinear terms in (19.3) can be exterminated. This
statement is known as Poincare-Dulac theorem20 . I will call “canonical” the coordi-
nates y in which the flow equation takes the form (19.7).

    Two remarks are in order. First, the above theorem does not suggest that the
global structure of the RG flow is in any sense “trivial”, even in non-resonance
cases. The series (19.4) which defines the transformation converges at best in some
finite neighborhood of the fixed point y = 0. Another interesting irregularities the
global RG flow may possess (like another fixed points) are located no closer then
at the boundary of this domain of convergence; in the new coordinates where B α ’s
are “trivial” they are “pushed out to the infinity”. To get some idea consider the
following
  20
    See e.g. V.I Arnold, Geometrical Methods in the Theory of Ordinary Differential Equations,
Springer, 1988


                                                     203
   Exercise: Study the flow equation with just one coordinate,
                                     d
                                        y = y − y2
                                     dl
(which has two fixed points y = 0 and y = 1). Find transformation y = f (ỹ) =
ỹ + · · · such that
                                    d
                                        ỹ = ỹ .
                                   dl
Study the properties of the map y → ỹ.

   Second remark concerns very simple interpretation which the above resonance
conditions have in terms of ordinary dimensional analysis. In the neighborhood of
A∗ where the coordinates y are defined, the action can be written as
                                     Z
                                   α
                       A = A∗ + y       dd x Φα (x) + O(y 2 ) ,

where Φα (x) are the fields which diagonalize the operator D associated with the
fixed point A∗ . The eigenvalues Dα - i.e. the dimensions of the fields Φα - are
related to κα as
                                  Dα + κα = d .
Therefore the numbers κα can be interpreted as the mass dimensions of the “coupling
constants” y α . The resonance condition (19.6) then states that the resonance term
(19.5) has “right dimension”. In this sense, the coordinate system in which the
RG flow equations have the form (19.7) respects the straightforward dimensional
analysis.

    From this point of view, what distinguishes the marginal case is that the presence
of a marginal field (i.e. the field Φα with Dα = d, or κα = 0) always leads to infinitely
many resonances. Indeed, if, say, κ0 = 0, the the resonance condition (19.6) with
any α can be satisfied by taking β1 = α and β2 = β3 = · · · = βn = 0.
   To illustrate this point let us consider an example with only two coupling con-
stants y 0 , y 1 , such that κ0 = 0 and κ1 = 2. I will use suggestive notations y 0 = λ
and y 1 = M 2 . Then in the flow equation for λ all terms λn are resonant, so the
coordinates exist in which this equation takes the form
                                   ∞
                            d     X
                               λ=     bn λn = −β(λ) .                             (19.8)
                            dl    n=2


                                          204
Similarly, in the equation for M 2 all terms of the form M 2 λn are resonant, and in
canonical coordinates one can write
                            ∞
               d 2      2
                           X
                  M = 2M −     γn M 2 λn = (2 − γφ2 (λ)) M 2 .                 (19.9)
               dl          n=1

Note that with this notations these equations have exactly the form of the coupling
and the mass “flow” equations associated with the Callan-Symanzik equation of the
d = 4 φ4 theory.
   Remark: In fact, in this important example a freedom still remains for further
changes of coordinates λ → λ̃(λ), M 2 → M̃ 2 = M 2 f (λ), which doesn’t change the
above “canonical” form of the flow equations.

    Exercise: Check (to the order n = when you get bored, or see the pattern) that
all terms ∼ λn with n ≥ 4 can be exterminated order by order, by suitable choice
of coefficients an in
                           λ̃ = λ + a3 λ3 + a4 λ4 + · · · .
Further, check that suitable redefinition of M 2 → M̃ 2 ,

                          M̃ 2 = M 2 1 + c1 λ + c2 λ2 + · · ·
                                                                


allows one to eliminate the terms with n ≥ 2 in the r.h.s. of (19.9). In reference
to renormalization of the φ4 theory at d = 4, these results imply that a scheme
exists in which all nth order corrections to the beta-function vanish starting from
n = 4, and all corrections to γφ2 vanish starting from n = 2. At the same time the
coefficients b2 and b3 , and γ1 , are scheme-independent.

    Note that the statement of the Poincare-Dulac theorem concerns with formal
power series. Existence of “canonical coordinates”, even locally, depends on conver-
gence of the series which in general is not guaranteed. For general RG flow in the
full space Σ the situation is indeed difficult. One of the problems lays again in the
“resonance denominators” κα − κβ1 − · · · − κβn which the coefficients a in the series
(19.4) have. When both positive and negative κα are present, even if no resonance
conditions (19.6) are satisfied exactly, they often are “almost satisfied”, in which
case some denominators come very close to zero giving rise to anomalously large
coefficients in (19.4).
   The situation becomes much more favorable if, instead of the flow in the full
space Σ, we concentrate attention on the RG flow in an unstable manifold U (A∗ )

                                          205
associated with some fixed point A∗ . First, U (A∗ ) typically has finite dimensionality
(as our study of A∗,G illustrates). Also, by the definition of unstable manifold all
corresponding κ are either positive (relevant) or zero (marginal). In this case the
resonance denominators are under much better control and the series (19.4) defining
the canonical coordinates usually converges in finite neighborhood of A∗ . Recall that
U (A∗ ) is a part of Σ(∞), i.e. it describes finite local QFT. For local QFT generated
by the fixed point A∗ the canonical coordinates do exist.

Callan-Symanzik equation revisited
The preceding analysis allows one to re-derive the Callan-Symanzik equation which
we have obtained before on the basis of renormalized perturbation theory.
    Let A∗ be a fixed point. Assuming that its unstable manifold is n-dimensional,
let {λi }, i = 1, · · · , n be coordinates on U (A∗ ), such that A∗ corresponds to λ = 0.
This unstable manifold describes n-parameter family of finite local field theories. I
will denote the corresponding correlation functions as

                                     ⟨φ(x1 ) · · · φ(xN )⟩λ ,

indicating their dependence on n parameters λi .
   In the coordinates λ the RG flow equation can be written as
                                    d i
                                       λ = −β i (λ) ,                               (19.10)
                                    dl
where −β i are components of the vector field B α on U (A∗ ) (by definition of U ,
B(U ) is tangent to U ), the minus sign is taken to match our previous notation for
the beta-function. According to the definition of the Wilson’s RG, if λ(l) is a solution
of (19.10) with some initial condition λ(0) = λ, the field theories corresponding to
λ and λ(l) are related by the scale transformation x → x/L. In particular, one can
write for the correlation functions
                                              N
                ⟨φ(x1 ) · · · φ(xN )⟩λ(l) = Z 2 (L) ⟨φ(Lx1 ) · · · φ(LxN )⟩λ(0) ,

where L = el . Taking infinitesimal transformation l = δl, L = 1 + δl, λ(l) =
λ − β(λ) δl, we have
                               n          

                               D(λ) = Dφ + γ(λ) ,

   The equation (19.11) generalizes straightforwardly to correlation functions in-

   We see that the CS equation is very general consequence of Wilson’s RG theory
and not just the property of the perturbation theory around the Gaussian fixed
