                                                 207
20      Fixed Points and Criticality
It is possible to establish direct relation between the singular behavior of thermody-
namic quantities near critical points and the fixed points of the Wilson’s RG. This
relation explains experimentally observed universality of critical singularities.
    Let us consider again a magnet. As we have seen few lectures ago, the long-range
fluctuations of local magnetization M (x) can be taken into account by considering
the statistical integral            Z
                               Z = [Dφ0 ] e−A0 [φ0 ] ,

where the field φ0 (x) is proportional to M (x),

                                φ0 (x) = C(T ) M (x) ,

with some temperature-dependent coefficient. It is assumed that this functional
integral has cutoff, with the cutoff parameter Λ such that

                                      Λ−1 >> a,

the atomic scale. Exact form of the action A0 is not known; it combines statistical
contributions of all microscopic fluctuations and depend significantly on all details
of the microscopic interactions. In landau-Ginsburg approximation one takes

                                            m20 (T ) 2 λ0 (T ) 4
                                                                
                                 1
                       Z
                            d            2
                 A0 =      d x     (∂φ0 ) +         φ0 +      φ0 ,
                                 2             2         4!

where m20 (T ), λ0 (T ) are unknown functions of T , but it can be any other combination
of powers of φ0 and its derivatives. What is important, all the coefficients are some
(generally unknown) functions of the temperature T . This means that the actions
describing real physical system, with all its microscopic details, being functions of
the temperature, form one-dimensional submanifold in Σ; I will call it the “physical
curve” (Wilson used the term “canonical curve”).
    Now let us assume that there is a fixed point A∗ out there in Σ. At this point we
actually will consider a subspace Σsymm ⊂ Σ which contains only actions invariant
under φ0 → −φ0 . We assume that there is a fixed point A∗ ∈ Σsymm such that
it has only one (symmetric) relevant (or marginally relevant) field Φ0 , with dimen-
sion D0 < d. In other words, the unstable manifold in Σsymm is one-dimensional,
and correspondingly the critical surface Σc has codimension one. This means that


                                          208
the physical curve generally intersects this critical surface at some point. This is
illustrated in the picture below.


                                              U( A )                   T>Tc
                                                  *


                                                            Tc
                                      A
                                          *
                      Σc                                              T<Tc




    Let us denote Tc the temperature corresponding to the intersection point. The
large-distance properties of the system can be understood if we can follow the RG
flow which starts at any point of the physical curve. Let us consider a typical pattern
of the RG flow in this situation. Besides the critical fixed point A∗ , there are two
non-critical (Rc = 0) fixed points, Phigh and Plow 21 .

                             P high

                                                                      T>Tc


                             A
                                 *             C                 Tc
                 Σc
                                                       Cτ

                           P low


     A trajectory starting at any point on the physical curve corresponding to T >
Tc , and far enough from Tc , converges quickly to the noncritical fixed point Phigh .
Similarly, starting at any point with T < Tc one quickly reaches Plow . The only
trajectory which behaves qualitatively differently is the one that starts at the point
Tc itself. This trajectory C lays entirely at the critical surface, and converges to the

critical fixed point A∗ as l → ∞. This means that the large-distance physics exactly
at the critical temperature is completely determined by the fixed-point action A∗ .
In particular, the two-point correlation function will have the scale-invariant form
                                                        Const

for |x − x′ | >> a el0 , where l0 is some finite amount of the RG “time” l needed to

   Consider now a trajectory Cτ which starts at some point T very close to Tc ,
                                     Tc − T
                                τ=          << 1 .                               (20.1)
                                       Tc
To be definite I will assume that T < Tc . There are three distinct stages of the RG
“evolution” for such trajectory. At the first stage it goes very close to C approaching
A∗ , until it comes to close proximity of this fixed point. It then stays near A∗ for
very long RG “time” l. This is the second stage. Finally, in the third stage it departs
toward Plow staying close to the unstable manifold U .
    The second stage is the most important one; that is where the critical singularities
are formed. Because at this stage the trajectory stays close to the fixed point, we
can use linear analysis. Let us assume that in some finite amount l0 of RG “time”
the trajectory Cτ comes close to A∗ , where the corresponding action can be written
as
                             Z                X        Z
                                  d
                A = A∗ + y0     d x Φ0 (x) +      yα      dd x Φα (x) ,          (20.2)
                                                  α̸=0

where the constants y depend on τ , the deviation (20.1) from the critical point. It is
important to note that this dependence is analytic because it comes from integrating
the RG flow equations for finite “time” l0 . As for τ = 0 one must stay at the critical
surface, which in the linear approximation corresponds to y0 = 0, for small τ we can
assume

                             y0 = Const × (Tc − T ) ,                            (20.3)

and neglect the τ -dependence of all yα with α ̸= 0.
   In the second stage the RG flow is described by the equations
                          d                       d

                          dl                      dl

                   κ0 = d − D0 > 0 ,       κα = d − Dα < 0 for α ̸= 0 .

During this second stage all irrelevant terms in (20.2) die out, while the coefficient
y0 increases as

                                    y0 (l) = y0 eκ0 (l−l0 ) .                               (20.4)

Recall that if Rc is the correlation length corresponding to the initial action on
the physical curve, the correlation length corresponding to the time l along the
trajectory is
                                  Rc (l) = Rc e−l .

and from the linear dependence (20.3)

                                       Rc ∼ |Tc − T |−ν .

Thus, the correlation length diverges near Tc , with the exponent determined by the
dimension D0 of the relevant field Φ0 . This divergence is in full agreement with
the idea that it is the large-scale fluctuations which are responsible for the critical
behavior.
    Similar way one can relate other critical exponents to the dimensions of various
fields at the fixed point A∗ . For example, the spontaneous magnetization

                                            M̄ ∼ ⟨φ0 ⟩

(nonzero in the low-T phase) must satisfy the CS equation, which in this case reads
                                            
                                          ∂
                             Dφ − κ0 y0        M̄ = 0 ,
                                         ∂y0
   Observe that within the linear approximation (20.2) the ratio Rc (l)/y0−ν (l) is a constant inde-

pendent of l.


                                                211
i.e.
                                             Dφ

                                      M̄ ∼ y0κ0 .                              (20.6)

                                     M̄ ∼ (Tc − T )β ,

where
                                             Dφ
                                      β=          .
                                           d − D0

   It is useful to note that the relations (20.5), (20.6) can be obtained by simple
dimensional counting, if one assumes that the coupling constant y0 has the dimension
κ0 and the field φ0 has the dimension Dφ . This follows from the CS equation with
the linear approximation for the RG functions β; in fact it is valid beyond the linear
approximation, as it follows from the existence of “canonical coordinates”.
   As another example of this dimensional counting let us find some other critical
exponents in terms of dimensions. If, at T = Tc , one adds an external field, i.e. the
term                                Z
                                H      dd x φ0 (x) ,

the dimensional counting implies that H has dimension d − Dφ , and so the magne-
tization as the function of H must have the following singular behavior
                                                  1
                                        M̄ ∼ H δ ,

where
                                           d − Dφ
                                      δ=          .
                                             Dφ
       Similar way one finds for the specific heat at H = 0

                                      C ∼ |Tc − T |α

with

                                     α = 2 − dν .                              (20.7)

Note that all these exponents are expressed through just two numbers, the dimen-
sions of the fields φ and Φ0 . Therefore no matter what the values of this dimensions

                                           212
are, the critical exponents must obey certain scaling relations, the relation (20.7)
being one example. These relation are confirmed in experiment.

    Note that the above analysis relies on the properties of the fixed point A∗ , as
well as on general topological properties of RG. Very little indeed depends on the
details of the original actions on the “physical curve”, and hence on the details of the
microscopic structure of the magnet. One expects therefore that the most important
characteristics of the critical singularities must not be different for phase transitions
in different magnetic materials (with the same symmetry). One fixed point may
represent the criticality in the whole class of physical systems. It is said it describes
a universality class of critical behavior. In fact, systems of very different physical
nature can belong to the same universality class. For example, the liquid-gas critical
point is likely to represent the same universality class as the phase transition in axial
ferromagnet.

    This all shows that understanding the fixed points is of crucial importance both
in statistical mechanics and in quantum field theory. Unfortunately, analysis and
classification of all fixed points are not yet achieved. In fact, at present very few
nontrivial fixed points can be analyzed on a systematic level.




                                          213
21      Wilson-Fisher Fixed Point
Previously, we have studied the simplest example of fixed point - the Gaussian
fixed point corresponding to free massless scalar field. It is easy to argue, however,
that the Gaussian fixed point cannot be responsible for the ordinary transition in
a magnet in three dimensions (the Gaussian fixed point describes the so called
“tricritical behavior”, as we will briefly discuss later). Indeed, it was very important
for the arguments given in the previous lecture that the critical surface of A∗ has
codimension one in Σsymm , i.e. that there is only one relevant field which respects
the symmetry of the problem (φ → −φ in our case). However, as we have seen, the
gaussian fixed point has more then one symmetric relevant fields for all d < 4. In
particular, for d = 3 two fields, Φ2 ∼ φ2 and Φ4 ∼ φ4 , are relevant. Therefore there
must be another fixed point describing the universality class of the magnetic phase
transition.
     The best theoretical evidence for the existence of another nontrivial fixed point
at d = 3 (on a formal level, at all 2 < d < 4) was given by Wilson and Fisher, and
it is usually referred to as the Wilson-Fisher fixed point A∗W F .
    At d < 4 the topology of the RG flow relating the fixed points A∗G and A∗W F is
likely to be the following. The unstable manifold U (A∗G ) at d < 4 has dimensionality
two; it is generated by the two relevant fields Φ2 and Φ4 (again, we restrict attention
to the symmetric space Σsymm ). The fixed point A∗W F is likely to lay in U (A∗G ), so
that the RG flow on this unstable manifold would look qualitatively as follows
                           2
                       M



                A ,G                                  A , WF           d<4
                 *                          C          *
                                  λ
                                                               Σc




   According to this picture there is a trajectory C connecting the fixed point A∗G
to A∗W F . Therefore A∗G lays on the critical surface Σc which also passes through
A∗W F . The unstable manifold U (A∗W F ) is then one-dimensional, as expected.


                                          214
    Note that the whole unstable manifold U (A∗G ) in this picture, being an element
of Σ(∞), represent local field theories. Namely, it represents two-parameter family
of φ4 field theories. These field theories can be characterized by two parameters, the
renormalized mass parameter M 2 and the renormalized coupling constant λ. There-
fore these parameters can be taken for coordinates in the above picture. Although
there is an ambiguity in the choice of these coordinates (the scheme dependence),
the critical surface corresponding to M 2 = 0 is defined by the scheme-independent
condition

                                      Γ(2) (p2 )|p2 =0 = 0 .                                 (21.1)

In these coordinates the fixed point A∗W F lays at M 2 = 0 and λ = λ∗ , where the
value of λ∗ depends on the scheme.

    We can formally consider the φ4 theory in d dimensional space, treating d as
continuous parameter. What happens when d approaches 4? We know that at d = 4
the field φ4 becomes marginally irrelevant, and for d > 4 it is strictly irrelevant, and
so for all d ≥ 4 the gaussian fixed point has only one relevant field φ2 . This behavior
emerges naturally if one assumes that when d approaches the value 4 from below
the fixed point A∗W F gets very close to the gaussian fixed point, and at d = 4 these
two fixed point merge. At d > 4 λ∗ becomes negative, and it does not correspond
to field theory in usual sense 23 . This idea leads to the patterns of the RG flow at
d = 4 and at d > 4 is shown in the pictures below.

                            2
                        M



                                                   A , WF              A ,G
                                                    *                   *
                                         λ




                                d=4                                                        d>4

  Note that the pattern at d = 4 is exactly what one expects to see when φ4 is
marginally irrelevant, in which case we know that the flow in the vicinity of the

   On the basis of these arguments Wilson and Fisher have assumed that if one
formally takes d = 4 − ϵ with ϵ small, say d = 3.99, the fixed point A∗W F can
be observed within the framework of renormalized perturbation theory in φ4 field
theory.

   Let us briefly discuss what changes in the renormalized perturbation theory of
φ4 if one takes d = 4 − ϵ instead of d = 4. As before, we start with the action in its
“bare” form

    It is worth stressing here that for d < 4 the diagrams describing the field and
the coupling constant renormalizations do not diverge; the only divergent renormal-
ization is the one of the mass parameter. Therefore the counterterms δZ and δλ
have finite limit when the cutoff parameter Λ goes to infinity. Therefore at d < 4

                                         216
these renormalizations are introduced not for the purpose of exterminating the di-
vergences, which are absent, but in order to introduce more suitable coordinate λ
on U (A∗G ) instead of λ0 . Although for d < 4 (unlike the case of d = 4) the bare
coupling λ0 can be used as local coordinate on this unstable manifold, we will see
soon that it is not too useful for the purpose of studying the fixed point A∗W F .

    As the fixed point A∗W F is expected to be located on the critical surface, it
suffices to study the massless φ4 theory which is defined by the condition (21.1),
                                 Γ(2) (p2 )|p2 =0 = 0 .

   This fixed point was obtained in the leading order of the renormalized pertur-

    The above result was obtained using renormalized perturbation theory for d < 4
 4
φ theory. As I mentioned, for d < 4 the unrenormalized perturbation theory, i.e.
the expansion in terms of the bare coupling constant λ0 , is (essentially) finite. It
is instructive to use this example to explain the relation between renormalized and
unrenormalized perturbation theories.
   If we repeat our calculation of the β-function using unrenormalized perturbation
theory, i.e. with the expression (21.8) for the four-point vertex, we would obtain
from (21.6)
                                 β0 (λ0 ) = −ϵ λ0 ,                               (21.10)
with no fixed point visible. Higher order corrections do not change this result; in fact,
it is easy to see that in unrenormalized perturbation theory, without the counterterm
contributions, the formula (21.10) is exact. If one uses λ0 as the coordinate on
U (A∗G ), the RG function β reduces to its linear part. The bare coupling constant
λ0 is the “canonical coordinate”, whose existence is guaranteed by the Poincare-
Dulac theorem.
   Indeed, in the case ϵ << 1 the RG flow equation for small values of the renor-
malized parameter λ has the form
                              (4π)2 d
                                       λ = λ (λ∗ − λ) .                           (21.11)
                                3 dl
It is not difficult to check that the change of variables
                                            λ0

                                           220
linearizes this flow equation,
                                   d
                                      λ0 = ϵ λ0 .                            (21.13)
                                   dl
Note that the transformation (21.12), or equivalently
                                    1   1   1
                                      =   +   ,
                                    λ   λ0 λ∗
maps the interval 0 < λ < λ∗ to the whole positive half-axis of λ0 ,

                           λ ∈ [0, λ∗ )   ↔     λ0 ∈ [0, ∞) ,

with the fixed point λ∗ mapped to the point λ0 = ∞; for this reason it is invisible
in (21.13). Note also that if one expands λ in terms of λ0 according to (21.12), for
small ϵ the coefficients of this expansion become large,
                                          λ20
                                 λ = λ0 −     + ··· ,
                                          λ∗
which explains the large coefficients in (21.8). For ϵ = 0 (i.e. d = 4) the inter-
val [0, λ∗ ) shrinks to zero, the perturbation becomes marginal, and the “canonical
coordinate” λ0 ceases to exist.

   If the renormalized parameter λ is used, for small ϵ the flow between A∗G and
A∗W F , as well as the fixed point A∗W F itself, remains at small λ ∼ ϵ, within the
domain where the perturbative results are reliable. Therefore for small ϵ we can use
the renormalized perturbation theory to calculate all characteristics of the Wilson-
Fisher fixed point. For instance, to the leading order in ϵ the flow equation for the
mass parameter M 2 has the same form as at d = 4,
                                                    
                       d 2              λ

picture above.

ϵ-expansion
The main assumption of ϵ-expansion is that the general features of the RG flow
observed at small ϵ remain qualitatively the same at ϵ = 1, i.e. at d = 4. Under this

                                          221
assumption, the characteristics of the Wilsin-Fisher fixed point can be calculated
perturbatively, as the power series in ϵ. Although at d = 3 the expansion parame-
ter ϵ is not small, this approach yields remarkably accurate results for the critical
exponents in three dimensions.
    To find the critical exponents one must calculate the dimensions of various rele-
van fields at the Wilson-Fisher fixed point. Note that Φ0 = φ2 is the only symmetric
relevant field at A∗W F . The dimensions of the fields Φ0 and φ at this point can be
calculated as
                             D0 = Dφ2 = d − 2 + γφ2 (λ∗ ) ,
                                      d−2
                                        Dφ =+ γ(λ∗ ) .
                                        2
In the leading order in ϵ we can just borrow the functions γ from the d = 4 calcu-
lation,
                                          λ
                             γφ2 (λ) =       + O(λ2 ) ,
                                       (4π)2
                                               1 λ2
                                     γ(λ) =            + O(λ3 ) ,
                                              12 (4π)4
and use the value (19.7) for λ∗ . This yields for the critical exponents
                                   1               ϵ
                                     = d − D0 = 2 − + O(ϵ2 ) ,
                                   ν               3
                                       d       ϵ
                                  α=2−      = + O(ϵ2 ) ,
                                    d − D0    6
and similar results for other exponents. For ϵ = 1 this gives ν = 0.60 and α = 0.16
for the correlation length and heat capacity exponents, to be compared to ν = 0.63
and α = 0.12 found in experiment 25 .

22      Nonlinear Sigma Model at d = 2 + ϵ
Now I would like to discuss how the RG transformation acts on the action of the
σ-model at d = 2. In this case the coupling constant g in
                                                  1
                                        Z
                                                        2

is dimensionless. I assume that g is small, so that the fluctuations of n(x) are small,
since at small g the exponential factor exp(−A) strongly suppresses configurations
having large gradients ∂n. I remind that the field n(x) satisfies the non-linear
constraint n2 (x) = 1.
    In principle, it is not difficult to develop renormalized perturbation theory in
the coupling constant g, derive the CS equation and find the series expansion of
associated flow function β(g). This calculation is displayed in § 13.3 of PS. I will
discuss the derivation based explicitly on the idea of integrating out the fast modes
(also described in § 13.3 of PS), because it has some novel features as compared to
what we have done before.
    First, we introduce cutoff,
                                                        d2 k
                                       Z
                             n(x) =            nk eikx       .
                                        |k|<Λ          (2π)2
Note that now   the modes nk are not all independent, but must obey complicated
constraint q nq nk−q = δ (2) (k), because of the σ-model constraint n2 (x) = 1.
           R

   Next, we want to split n(x) into its “slow” and “fast” components. The above
nonlinear constraint makes this task less straightforward then we had it before in
usual scalar theories. Physically transparent way to make the splitting is as follows.
We define the “slow” component n0 (x) as the field of unit length,
                                     n20 (x) = 1 ,                                 (22.1)
whose Fourier transform is restricted to |k| < Λ/L. To make further arguments
transparent, I will assume that L >> 1; we will see that in this case the dominating
contribution to the RG transformed action comes from the integration over the fast
modes with Λ/L << |k| << Λ. More precisely, I will assume that L >> 1, but g is
so small that
                                   g log L << 1 .
    At each spatial point x, one can choose a system of vectors ei (x), i = 1, 2, ..., N −
1, such that N vectors (n0 (x), ei (x)) form a basis. It is convenient to take orthonor-
mal basis,
                                                        
                     n0 (x)ei (x) = 0 ,      ei (x)ek (x) = δik .                   (22.2)

                                           227
Since ei (x) only need to be orthogonal to the “slow” field n0 (x), one can always
choose them to be “slow” fields as well, containing only fourier modes with |k| of
the order of Λ/L or smaller. The full field n(x) can be written as
                                p
                   n(x) = n0 (x) 1 − (ϕi )2 (x) + ei (x) ϕi (x) .            (22.3)

This expression automatically satisfies the constraint n2 (x) = 1. N − 1 fields ϕi (x)
represent the fast modes; they contain fourier components with |k| between Λ/L
and Λ. It is important that when g is small we can assume ϕi (x) are small.
   In order to compute the action, we need spatial derivatives of n(x). These involve
the derivatives of the vectors n0 (x) and ei (x), as well as of the fields ϕi . One can
expand them in the basic vectors as

                          ∂µ n0 (x) = Bµi (x) ei (x) ,                          (22.4)

                                       µ (x) ek (x) − Bµ (x) n0 (x) ,           (22.5)

                              Bµi (x) = ei ∂µ n0 = − n0 ∂µ ei ,

   As this gauge transformation describes just the freedom in the choice of variables,

                                 Aij ij         t

However it involves extra derivative, and thus corresponds to an irrelevant term.

   Exercise: For the O(N ) nonlinear sigma model,
   I. At d = 2
   a) Assuming g(l) << 1, find Zn (l).
   b) Derive the following |x| → 0 asymptotic of the two-point correlation function

   II. Find the flow equation for g(l) and Zn (l) at d = 2 + ϵ, with ϵ << 1. Again,
determine the |x| → 0 behavior of the two-point correlation function.

   Coming back to the flow equation (d = 2)

                                 dg   N −2 2
                                    =     g + ...
                                 dl    2π
one observes that (at N > 2) the flow of the coupling g(l) looks like this:

   0
                                                                         g


   At large distances g increases, and the interaction becomes strong. Our weak
coupling analysis is no longer valid (in particular the higher order terms in β(g) can

                                          234
not be neglected). On the other hand, if we follow the flow of g(l) backward, to
l → −∞,                                 −1
                                N −2               2π
                 g(l) = Const −        l     →          (−l)−1 ,
                                  2π             N −2
the interaction of the Goldstone modes become weaker, and the short-distance be-
havior is controlled by a free theory. For this reason, this type of RG flow is called
asymptotic freedom. Note that the situation is opposite to that in φ2 theory at d = 4,
where instead λ → 0 at long scales, whereas perturbation theory breaks down at
short distances.
    The asymptotic freedom allows one to establish existence of local field theory;
indeed it shows that at short scales the fluctuations are suppressed, and the cutoff
can be removed with no violation of locality. On the other hand, at longer scales
the fluctuations become stronger. It is not possible to describe these large scale
fluctuations by perturbation theory. Nonetheless, one can make intuitive picture
based on this pattern of the RG flow. If one concentrate attention on very short
scales, the vectors n(x) are nearly parallel, because at l → −∞ g(l) is small,
thus suppressing the gradients of n(x). However, if one looks at larger scales, the
directions of n(x) at the points separated by distance ∼ Rc are much less correlated,
and if the separation id even much larger, there is no correlation.




        short scale                                               large scale

   This picture suggests that at d = 2 the phase with spontaneously broken O(N )
symmetry does not exist. In other words, d = 2 magnet has no ferromagnetic phase
with spontaneous magnetization. Let me stress that the perturbative analysis of
the RG flow does not prove this statement, which requires much more involved


                                         235
arguments. Note also that this picture is expected to apply only if N > 2 28 .
    What is different at d > 2? This question can be studied in details in formal
case of d = 2 + ϵ with ϵ << 1. In this case we can repeat our calculation of the RG
flow at small g. The step 1 (elimination of the fast part) yields essentially the same
result,
                       Z                        
                             1    N −2 ϵ
                 A1 =           −       Λ log L (∂µ n0 )2 d2 x .                 (22.9)
                            2g     4π

However, the step 2 (scale transformation) is no longer exactly trivial. The change
of variables
                                  n0 (x) = n(x/L)
leads to                   Z                                  Z
                                            2    d      d−2
                               (∂µ n0 ) d x = L                   (∂µ n)2 dd x .

Therefore                                                 
                                            1   N −2 ϵ
                           Z

                                           2g    4π

                                                Lϵ ≈ 1 + ϵ log L
one finds that under the RG transformation
                                             
                         1     1     ϵ N −2
                           →     +     −        log L ,
                         g     g     g     2π
  28
    At N = 2 the g 2 term in the β-function vanishes. In fact, it is easy to see that all higher
terms vanish as well. At N = 2 the direction n(x) can be parameterized by a single angle θ(x),
n(x) = cos θ(x), sin θ(x) , and formally

                                           1
                                             Z
                                     A=        (∂µ θ)2 d2 x ,
                                          2g
which is just the action of free massless theory. The action of RG on a free theory is trivial.
However, beyond the perturbation theory, the N = 2 sigma model has more complex structure.
This is because the field θ(x) is an angular variable, θ(x) ∼ θ(x) + 2π N (x), where N (x) is
integer-valued. As the result, there are topologically nontrivial field configurations (”vortices”,
or ”instantons”). Their interaction leads to remarkable phase transition at finite g = gc - the
so called Berezinsky - Kosterlitz - Thouless transition (See e.g. Kosterlitz, Thouless, ”Ordering,
metastability and phase transitions in two-dimensional systems”, Journal of Physics C: Solid State
Physics, Vol. 6 pages 1181-1203 (1973)).

where I have ignored the factor Λϵ ≈ 1 29 . The associated RG flow equation is

Due to the small linear term, the flow now looks different at small g ∼ ϵ,

      GS                       g

   Nontrivial new fixed point at g = g∗ appears, and if g < g∗ the theory flows
towards the Goldstone fixed point at large scale.
   It is natural to identify the new fixed point g∗ with the Wilson-Fisher fixed point
at d = 2 + ϵ. Indeed, general pattern of RG flow in O(N ) φ4 theory suggested by
our previous analysis was as follows


               M2
                                                       A* WF
      A* G                                                              λ

                                                       g−g
                                                          *

                                                       AGS

    Here M 2 and λ are two couplings in φ4 theory. At closely below 4 the Wilson-
Fisher fixed point appears close to A∗,G , while the Goldstone fixed point is located
far away from it. When d goes down from 4 A∗,G departs from A∗,W F , but A∗,GS
gets closer.
 29
      One can get rid of this factor by changing to a dimensionless coupling g → Λ−ϵ g.

    We can identify the coordinate along the trajectory going from A∗,W F to A∗,GS
with the coupling g of the non-linear sigma model, with the origin g = 0 at A∗,GS ;
then A∗,W F appear at some g∗ . At d = 2 + ϵ with small ϵ A∗,GS approaches A∗,W F
(g∗ ∼ ϵ). At d = 2 the fixed points A∗,GS and A∗,W F merge, and the flow of g (at
positive g) becomes marginally relevant. This pattern is confirmed by exact solution
of the theory at N → ∞ (partly described in §13.3 of PS).




                                          238
23         RG and Energy-Momentum Tensor
Renormalization Group describes the way the scale transformations are realized in
QFT. The scale transformations

                                         xµ → x̃µ = xµ /L

constitute particular class of general coordinate transformations

                                        xµ → x̃µ = f µ (x) ,

where f µ are arbitrary smooth functions. Variations of the action functional 30

                                               Z
                                     A=−           dd x L(x)                          (23.1)

under these coordinate transformations in a local field theory is generally described
in terms of its energy-momentum tensor T µν (x). Therefore this field, and in partic-
ular its trace
                                 Θ(x) := Tµµ (x) ,
plays important role in RG theory. In fact, all basic equations of RG theory in local
QFT can be expressed as certain properties of this field.

    In the first lecture of this course we have defined the Energy-Momentum tensor
(or EM tensor, for shortness), in a classical field theory, as the Noether’s current
associated with the translational symmetry xµ → xµ + aµ ,
                                              ∂L
                                    Tνµ =           ∂ν φ − δνµ L ,
                                            ∂(∂µ φ)

which satisfies the continuity equation

                                            ∂µ Tνµ = 0                                (23.2)
  30
       The sign minus in this formula corresponds to the relation
                                Z              Z                  Z
                 −A = iS = i      dt dx L(x) =    dx4 dx L(x) = −   dx4 dx LE (x) .

where L(x) is the usual Lagrangian density with t replaced by −ix4 , see Lecture 7.



                                                   239
as a consequence of the classical equations of motion. Generally, there is an impor-
tant ambiguity in the definition of the local EM tensor. One can redefine Tνµ by
adding a total derivative

                          Tνµ → Tνµ + ∂λ ψνµλ ,     ψνµλ = −ψνλµ .

The new tensor still satisfies the continuity equation (23.2). By appropriate choice
of ψ the modyfied EM tensor can always be made symmetric, T µν = T νµ .
   More general way to define the EM tensor is to consider the field theory in the
space with nontrivial metric

                                ds2 = gµν (x) dxµ dxν .                          (23.3)

In this case the action
                                                √
                                        Z
                          A[φ, g] = −       dd x g L(φ, ∂µ φ, gµν )

becomes a functional of the metric tensor g also.
   For instance, for generic scalar field theory, in the presence of the metric back-
ground (23.3), the simplest action is
                                                           
                                √ 1 µν
                             Z
                       A=         g     g ∂µ φ∂ν φ + V (φ) .
                                      2
This form is called the “minimal coupling to gravity”.
   In any case, the field T µν (x) is defined in terms of the variation of the action
with respect to the metric (sign!),
                              1          √
                                 Z
                      δA = −         dd x g T µν (x) δgµν (x) .                (23.4)
                              2
Note that T µν defined this way is automatically symmetric,

                                    T µν (x) = T νµ (x) ,

as gµν is.
    Note that for flat space this definition is still ambiguous, as one could couple the
theory to the external metric in many different ways. For example, in the scalar
field theory one could add the “non-minimal” terms like

               d √                                     √
           Z                                  Z
              d x g R W (φ) ,         or          dd x g R[µλ][νρ] W [µλ][νρ] (φ)

                                            240
where R is the curvature, and W is arbitrary. These terms disappears in the flat
space, however it affects the variation (23.4) even when the metric is set back to the
flat Euclidean one. It is possible to show that all such “non-minimal” modifications
of the action in curved space lead to modifications of the EM tensor of the form

                            T µν → T µν + ∂λ ∂ρ Y [µλ][νρ] ,                       (23.5)

where Y is antisymmetric in [µλ] and in [νρ] but symmetric w.r.t. the interchange of
these pairs (this is exactly the symmetry of the Riemann’s curvature tensor R[µλ][νρ] ).
   This is intrinsic ambiguity which cannot be resolved unless we specify exactly
how the system behaves in curved space. Note that if Y decays sufficiently fast at
x → ∞, such modifications do not affect the notions of energy and momentum,
                            Z                       Z
                                 d−1 00         i
                       E=      d xT ,         P =     dd−1 x T 0i ,

as they add total derivatives to the integrands. If we are interested just in the theory
in flat metric, fixing this ambiguity it is the matter of convenience.

   Consider now quantum field theory. The correlation functions are defined through
the functional integrals
                                          Z
                                       −1
         ⟨O1 (x1 ) · · · On (xn )⟩ = Z      [Dφ] O1 (x1 ) · · · On (xn ) e−A[φ,g] , (23.6)

where for the moment I consider the system in external gravitational field gµν (x).
Here O(x) are some local fields, which can be the field φ(x) itself, or some composite
fields built from φ and its derivatives at the point x. In the presence of the external
metric these composite fields may depend on this metric in a local way, i.e. thay
may involve gµν (x) and its derivatives at the point x.

   Consider a small variation around the flat metric,

                                gµν (x) = δµν + δgµν (x) .

For generic variation the correlation functions change in a nontrivial way because
the theory indeed depends on the background metric. However, the change is simple
if we take δgµν (x) corresponding to pure coordinate transformation

                                  xµ → xµ + εµ (x)                                 (23.7)


                                            241
with infinitesimal εµ (x); in this case
                              −δgµν (x) = ∂µ εν (x) + ∂ν εµ (x) .
    The coordinate transformation is just a relabeling of the degrees of freedom φ(x),
so it amounts to a change of variables in the functional integral (23.6). Therefore
the numerical value of this integral does not change. This leads to the identity
                     n
                     X
                0=         ⟨O1 (x1 ) · · · δε Oi (xi ) · · · On (xn )⟩ +
                     i=1
                                  Z
                                       dd x ∂µ εν (x) ⟨T µν (x) O1 (x1 ) · · · On (xn )⟩ ,   (23.8)

where δε Oi (x) stand for local variations of the fields Oi (xi ) under the coordinate
transformation (23.7). The identities of this type are generally known as the Ward
identities.
    What can be said about the variations δε Oi (x) on general grounds? These again
are local fields which depend on εµ (x)
   (i) linearly, i.e. δε1 +ε2 O = δε1 O + δε2 O, and
   (ii) localy, i.e. δε O(x) depends on εµ (x) and its derivatives taken at the point x.
   In fact, the part of δε O(x) which contains just εµ (x), with no derivatives, is
completely fixed,
       δε O(x) = εµ (x) ∂µ O(x) + terms with ∂ν εµ (x) and higher derivatives ,
because for constant εµ (x) = εµ we have
                       O(x) → O(x + ε) = O(x) + εµ ∂µ O(x) .

    Moreover, the part which contains the first derivatives ∂ν εµ (x) is also severely
restricted. For instance, if φ(x) is a scalar field, and we assume that it transforms
undependently on other fields, we can write
                                               D
                     δε φ(x)(x) = εµ (x) ∂µ φ(x) +(∂µ εµ ) φ(x) ,
                                                d
where d is the dimensionality of the space, and the parameter D is related to the
dimension of φ. This generalizes to more complicated scalar composite fields Oα as
follows
                                         Dαβ
     δε Oα (x) = εµ (x) ∂µ Oα (x) +          (∂µ εµ ) Oβ (x) + terms with ∂ν ∂λ εµ , etc .
                                         d
                                                 242
For tensor fields the terms involving local rotations

                                 ωµν (x) = ∂µ εν (x) − ∂ν εµ (x)

will also appear.

   Going back to the Ward Identity (23.8), we observe that integral there can be
transformed by parts, hence we have
                      n
                      X
                 0=         ⟨O1 (x1 ) · · · δε Oi (xi ) · · · On (xn )⟩ −
                      i=1
                                   Z
                                       dd x εν (x) ⟨∂µ T µν (x) O1 (x1 ) · · · On (xn )⟩ .    (23.9)

As the first term here depends only on ε(x) (and its derivatives) at the points
x1 , · · · , xn only, we conclude that

               ⟨∂µ T µν (x) O1 (x1 ) · · · On (xn )⟩ = 0 for x ̸= x1 , · · · , xn ,

i.e. in quantum field theory the continuity equation (23.2) is satisfied in the weak
sense,
                                  ∂µ T µν (x) ≃ 0 ,
exactly as the equations of motion do.

   Now let us consider particular case of the coordinate transformations, the scale
transformations (also called the dilations)

                                         xµ → xµ + ε xµ .

In this case
                                         ∂µ εν (x) = δµν ε ,
while all higher derivatives of ε(x) vanish, and so for scalar fields Oα we have

                                    δε Oα (x) = ε (DO)α (x) ,

                                            ∂

Then the Ward identity (23.8) reduces to

      ⟨Oα1 (x1 ) · · · (DO)αi (xi ) · · · Oαn (xn )⟩ =        dd x⟨Θ(x)Oα1 (x1 ) · · · Oαn (xn )⟩ (23.11)

where
                                             Θ(x) = Tµµ (x)
is the trace component of the EM tensor.
    Now let us assume that our QFT depends on some number N of parameters (the

                                                 ∂

                                                N

                    n

                    i=1

where the coefficients Dαβ (λ) in the operator D in (23.10) can of course depend on
the parameters λ. The possibility to expand the field Θ(x) in terms of finitely many
“basic” fields Φk (x) is the statement of renormalizability of the field theory.
  31
     Of course, in general the fields Oα (x) may depend on the couplings λ. If such dependence is
present, the derivatives of Oα would add to the matrix Dαβ , but otherwise would not change our
argument. Here I ignore this contribution for simplicity.



                                                      244
    The energy-momentum tensor T µν (x) is special composite field. As we know, in
interacting field theory composite fields in general may require infinite renormaliza-
tions. However, the above derivation of the Ward identity (23.8) suggests that in the
case of the EM tensor no infinite renormalization is needed. Indeed, the correlation
functions in (23.8) are understood as the correlation functions of fully renormalized
fields Oα , they remain finite when the regularization is removed. Therefore the
EM tensor defined through the variation (23.4) must have finite correlation func-
tions without additional renormalization. It is possible to check this statement in
renormalized perturbation theory in d = 4 φ4 theory.

Conformal invariance at the fixed-point
If λk = λk∗ such that
                                    β k (λ∗ ) = 0 ,
the trace of the EM tensor vanishes in view of (23.12)

                                   Θ(x) = 0 .                                 (23.14)

    Remark: The above statement ignores important subtlety. The derivation of
the Callan-Symanzik equation (23.13) relies in fact on the integrated version of
(23.12). Locally, Eq.(23.12) may hold only up to total derivatives, i.e. the terms of
the form ∂µ K µ (x), with K µ (x) being some local field. Thus, vanishing of the beta-
functions only implies that Θ(x) = ∂µ K µ (x). Unless K µ is itself a total derivative
(i.e. Θ = ∂µ ∂ν L, in which case the ambiguity (23.5) can be used to redefine Tµν to
eliminate its trace), presence of such a term would spoil the argument for conformal
invariance. In some cases (in particular, in d = 2 unitary quantum field theories)
this possibility can be excluded. Generally it is not excluded, and therefore van-
ishing of the beta-functions does not guarantee conformal invariance. Moreover, in
general, counterexamples can be found. For more on this subtlety, see J.Polchinski,
Renormalization and effective Lagrangians, Nucl.Phys. B231, 269 (1984). In the
following discussion I assume that the fixed point in question admits the EM tensor
which satisfies (23.14).
   The correlation functions of this fixed-point theory enjoy scale invariance with
the dimensions of the fields Oα determined by the eigenvalues of the matrix

                                   Dαβ = Dαβ (λ∗ ) .

In fact, vanishing of the trace component Θ(x) in the fixed-point theory guarantees
much bigger symmetry. Namely, let v µ (x) be any vector field which satisfies the

                                         245
equation
                                                               2
                   ∂µ vν (x) + ∂ν vµ (x) = ρ(x) δµν =            δµν (∂λ v λ (x)) .          (23.15)
                                                               d
Consider the coordinate transformation

                       xµ → xµ + εµ (x) with εµ (x) = ε v µ (x) ,                            (23.16)

where ε is infinitesimal parameter. For such εµ

                                     ∂µ εν T µν = ρ(x) Θ(x) = 0 ,

and the second term in the Ward identity (23.8) disappears, i.e.

                              i=1

We obtain the closed equation

and Φα (x) are the fields which diagonalize the matrix Dαβ , and Dα are corresponding

    The coordinate transformations (23.16) which satisfy the equation (23.15) are
called the conformal transformations. It is possible to find all solutions of this
equation. The situation turns out to be rather different in the cases d > 2 and
d = 2.
       If d > 2 the general solution has the form32

                     v µ (x) = aµ + ω µν xν + l xµ + (bµ x2 − 2 xµ (bx)) ,
  32
    The Lie algebra of these vector fields is isomorphic to so(d + 1, 1), the algebra of infinitesimal
Lorentz transformations of d + 2-dimensional space-time.

where ω µν = −ω νµ . The parameters a, ω, l, b are interpreted as follows:

                     aµ         − translations
                       ω µν     − rotations
                     l           − dilations
                     bµ         − special conformal transformations

   As the first three are very familiar, I will present only the finite version of the
special conformal transformations,
                                                xµ + B µ x2
                              xµ → x̃µ =                        ,
                                            1 + 2 (Bx) + B 2 x2
where B µ is an arbitrary vector.

   Invariance of the fixed-point theory w.r.t. the special conformal transformations
provides further limitations on the correlation functions. Consider the Ward identity
(23.18) with
                              v µ (x) = bµ x2 − 2 xµ (bx) .
In this case
                                                        
                             µ 2     µ      ∂
               Dv Φα (x) = (b x − 2 x (bx)) µ − 2 Dα (bx) Φα (x) ,
                                           ∂x

and (23.18) becomes nontrivial closed differential equation for the correlation func-
tions. In fact, this equation completely determines one- , two- and three-point
correlation functions, up to overall constant factors (Homework Problem 10)

   If d = 2 the equation

                              ∂µ vν + ∂µ vν − δµν (∂λ v λ ) = 0               (23.19)

has infinitely many solutions. This is very easy to see by going to the complex
coordinates in d = 2 space,

                              z = x1 + i x2 ,           z̄ = x1 − i x2 .

Then (23.19) leads to two equations

                                  ∂z̄ v z = 0 ,         ∂z v z̄ = 0 ,

                                                  247
which shows that v z (z) (v z̄ (z̄)) can be arbitrary holomorphic (anti-holomorphic)
function. This rich symmetry has important consequences for the structure of d = 2
conformal field theories.
   Scale invariance of the fixed-point theory allows one to establish some important
properties of QFT associated with its unstable manifold.

Scale invariance and density of states
Important conclusion can be made about its density of states. The density of states
is directly related to the equilibrium state entropy S(E) of the system. The form of
S(E) in a scale invariant quantum field theory can be established directly by general
arguments. However, I will use this opportunity to display some general features of
QFT at finite temperature.
    The functional integral of d-dimensional Euclidean quantum field theory
                                            Z
                                         −1
                             ⟨ ... ⟩ = Z      D[φ] (...) e−A[φ]

has formal interpretation as expectation value over a thermal equilibrium ensemble
in classical statistical mechanics in d spatial dimensions. We have used this inter-
pretation many times already. Here I would like to discuss Euclidean functional
integral representation of quantum statistical mechanics of fields in d dimensional
space-time (i.e. d − 1 dimensional space).
   Recall that the density matrix of thermal equilibrium state in quantum statistics
has the general form33

                                   P̂ = Z −1 (β) e−β Ĥ ,                               (23.20)

where β = (kT )−1 . The exponential here coincides with the time evolution operator
at pure imaginary value of time t = −iℏβ (I set ℏ = 1 henceforth). This form
is still valid if one is dealing with the equilibrium state of quantum fields. The
imaginary-time evolution operator has representation as the functional integral,
                                            Z
                          −β Ĥ
                ⟨ φf (x)|e      |φi (x) ⟩ =   D[φ(x, τ )] φ(x,0)=φi (x) e−A[φ] (23.21)
                                                            φ(x,β)=φf (x)


  33
    The normalization factor Z(β) here is different from the factor Z in e.g. Eq.(7.1). Z(β) goes
to Z in the low temperature limit β → ∞ (see below).



                                              248
Here x are d − 1 dimensional ”spatial” coordinates, and τ is the imaginary time (so
that x = (x, τ )). The integration is over the functions φ(x) = φ(x, τ ) in the slab
0 ≤ τ ≤ β, with the boundary conditions

                        φ(x, 0) = φi (x) ,         φ(x, β) = φf (x) .

   The partition sum of the quantum system at the finite temperature is the trace

                             Z(β) = tr e−β Ĥ ,
                                             
                                                                          (23.22)

i.e. the sum of its diagonal matrix elements
                         Z
                            D[φ(x)] ⟨ φ(x)| e−β Ĥ |φ(x) ⟩ .                   (23.23)

If the representation (23.21) is used, the integration over φ(x) in (23.23) combines
with the integral D[φ(x)] in (23.21) to produce integral over all fields φ(x, τ ), with
0 ≤ τ ≤ β, satisfying the periodic boundary condition

                                  φ(x, 0) = φ(x, β) .                          (23.24)

Thus, we have
                              Z
                     Z(β) =       D[φ(x, τ )]φ(x,0)=φ(x,β) e−A[φ] .            (23.25)

Geometrically, this is the functional integral over the functions φ(x) on a d-dimensional
cylinder Rd−1 × S 1 , with τ being the coordinate on the circle of circumference β,
τ ∼ τ + β. Of course this is nothing but the Matsubara representation specified to
the quantum field theory at hand.
    Now, let us change interpretation of the functional integral (23.25). We can take
one of the coordinates on Rd−1 , say x1 , and claim it to be the imaginary time. The
coordinate τ will be treated as one of the spatial coordinates. In this interpretation
the imaginary time spans infinite range, −∞ < x1 < ∞, while the ”space” is
Rd−2 × S 1 is the d − 1 dimensional cylinder, with the coordinates (x∥ , x⊥ ). Here
x∥ = (x2 , ..., xd−1 ), and x⊥ = τ . The later is compactified,

                                      x⊥ ∼ x⊥ + β .

    Since now the imaginary time spans large (in the limit, infinite) range, (23.25)
is dominated by the contribution from the ground state,

                                  Z(β) ∼ e−L E0 (β) ,                          (23.26)

                                             249
where L → ∞ is the range of x1 , and E0 (β) is the ground state energy of the
quantum field in the spatial geometry Rd−2 × S 1 .
    This geometric setting is similar to that we discussed in the context of the Casimir
effect, with β replacing the separation between the plates a. The difference is that
in the Casimir case we had a fixed boundary condition φ = 0 at the plates, which
now is replaced by the periodic boundary condition (23.24). For the free massless
field it is easy to repeat the calculation of the Casimir energy (I recommend it as
an Exercise), with the result

                                              C V∥
                                 E0 (β) = −         ,                             (23.27)
                                              β d−1

where C is some dimensionless (d-dependent) constant, and
                                      Z
                                 V∥ =   dd−2 x∥

is the volume of the Rd−2 component of the space. In fact, apart from the value of the
constant, the form (23.27) is dictated by scale invariance of the theory. Indeed, the
ground state energy is an extensive quantity, which at large V∥ must be proportional
to this volume. Since the theory has no intrinsic scale, the β-dependence then follows
from the dimensional analysis. This simple argument shows that the form (23.27)
applies to any scale-invariant field theory, id est fixed point field theory. The constant
C of course would be different for different fixed points.
    Returning to original interpretation - the quantum field at finite temperature
kT = β −1 , and using (23.26) we have for the free energy of a scale-invariant field
theory
                                                        C Vd−1
                        β F (β) = − log(Z(β)) = −              ,                  (23.28)
                                                         β d−1
where Vd−1 = V∥ ×L is the volume of the space. Of course, one could have established
the form (23.28) directly, by dimensional analysis (the free energy F , being extensive
characteristic, must be ∼ Vd−1 , whereas the quantity (23.28) is dimensionless), but
I believe our detour through the Casimir energy was instructive.
    The finite temperature partition function is closely related to the energy density
of states. We have                   Z ∞
                             Z(β) =       e−β E ρ(E) dE ,
                                        0




                                            250
where ρ(E) ∆E is the number of states in the energy interval between E and E+∆E.
Mathematically, Z(β) is the Laplace transform of ρ(E). The inverse transform is
                      Z +i∞                   Z +i∞
                             βE        dβ                  C Vd−1
                                                                  dβ
             ρ(E) =         e Z(β)         =         eβ E e βd−1      .
                       −i∞             2πi     −i∞                2πi

At large Vd−1 the integral is dominated by the saddle point at
                                                                1/d
                                                C (d − 1) Vd−1
                           β = β0 (E) :=                                ,
                                                      E

and we find

                                      ρ(E) ∼ eS(E) ,                                    (23.29)

where
                                                 1/d
                                 S(E) = C̃ Vd−1 E 1−1/d                                 (23.30)

where C̃ is another constant, related to C in (23.27)34 .
    By the definition, the quantity S(E) in (23.29) coincides with the equilibrium
state entropy of the system at the temperature kT = 1/β0 (E). The equation (23.30)
expresses the universal scale invariant form of the entropy. Indeed, the entropy is
an extensive property which must have the form Vd−1 σ, where the entropy den-
sity σ = σ(ε) can depend on the energy density ε := E/Vd−1 . On the other hand
S(E), being a dimensionless quantity, can only depend on the dimensionless combi-
nation Vd−1 E d−1 . Eq. (23.30) is the only form that satisfies these conditions. Thus,
the asymptotic density of states (23.29)(23.30) can be deduced directly, from the
scale invariance of the fixed point theory. Nonetheless, our previous discussion is
instructive as it displays some basics of the finite-temperature formalism of QFT.
   Note that in any dimensionality S(E) grows slower then the exponential ∼ E.
This observation is useful in comparing to the string theories, whose typical energy
density is the exponential35 .
   It is straightforward to argue that the energy density (23.29), (23.30) is valid
asymptotically, at large E, in any quantum field theory originating at certain critical
  34      d
    C̃ = d−1 (C (d − 1))1/d
  35
    Stringy density of states S(E) ≃ E/kTH has manifestation in the so called Hagedorn transition
at T = TH .


                                                251
fixed point (i.e. associated with U (A∗ ). And if we adopt the Wilsonian idea of
quantum field theories as RG flows on the unstable manifolds of fixed points, the
above equation (23.30) become general limitation on the asymptotic density of states
in quantum field theory.
    Let me make here a remark on the status of this idea. In our discussion of the
Renormalization Group we (following Wilson) found it natural to identify local field
theories with the special class of RG flows in Σ, the flows that can be ”integrated
backward” without limit. A critical fixed point A∗ gives simple mechanism for
generating such flows. There can be several fixed points, or even continuous manifold
of fixed points36 . In any case, the above conclusion about the high energy density
of states, which follows from the scale invariance of the fixed point theory, remains
valid.
    However, another, somewhat more intricate behavior is conceivable. Let me
briefly discuss it, just in the way of entertainment. To start with, let us imagine a
slice of the critical surface passing through a fixed point A∗ . All RG trajectories in
some domain around A∗ flow to A∗ at l → +∞, see Figure below. Let’s also assume
that there is another fixed point A′∗ laying in the same critical surface, such that
A∗ ∈ U (A′∗ ). Furthermore, assume there is yet another fixed point A′′∗ ∈ U (A′∗ ).

                        A*’’




                        A*’                                    A*




This Figure shows an RG trajectory which originates at A′′∗ , then passes very close
to A′∗ , finally converging to A∗ . Starting from any point on this trajectory, one can
”integrate backward” without limit, i.e. defines local QFT. While the large scale
 36
      About limit cycles, C-theorems, etc.


                                             252
properties of this QFT are controlled by the ”infrared” fixed point A∗ , its behavior
at shorter scales is more intricate. When traced backwards, the RG trajectory stays
long ”time” near the fixed point A′∗ , before departing towards A′′∗ at l → −∞.
The associated QFT has its asymptotic short distance behavior determined by the
”ultraviolet” fixed point A′′∗ , but in some wide range of intermediate scales it has
scaling properties associated with A′∗ .
    One can imagine yet more complicated sequence of nested fixed points, giving
rise to QFT with many different intermediate regimes corresponding to the trajec-
tory passing close to consecutive fixed points. Ultimately, then, a flow is conceivable
which passes successively near infinitely many fixed points. While such trajectory
can be integrated backward with no limit, its short distance behavior is not con-
trolled by any given fixed point.




                                         253
24      Operator Formalism vs Functional Integral in
        QFT
So far we have discussed QFT mostly from the perspective of (Euclidean space-
time) functional integral. There are aspects which are better understood within the
formalism based on the notions of the Hilbert space of states which supports the al-
gebra of local field operators. In the previous discussion I made occasional references
to statements that follow from the operator formalism, mostly from the positivity
of norms in the Hilbert space, as well as from the so called spectral condition. Here
I would like to recollect the basics of that formalism, and clarify its relation to the
functional integral.

Operator formalism
As was stated before (Lecture 7), the correlation functions defined by the functional
integrals
                                          Z
                                       −1
          ⟨ O1 (x1 )...ON (xN ) ⟩ := Z      [DΦ] O1 (x1 )...ON (xN ) e−A[Φ]    (24.1)

can be interpreted as the vacuum-vacuum matrix element of the ”T-ordered” prod-
uct
                                                                   
             ⟨ O1 (x1 )...ON (xN ) ⟩ = ⟨0 | Tτ Ô1 (x1 )...ÔN (xN ) | 0⟩ (24.2)

of the Heisenberg field operators Ô(x). The latter notions involve nominating one of
the coordinates to the role of the ”Euclidean time” τ , so that x = (x, τ ). Usually, the
Heisenberg picture is developed in the real time t which relates to τ by the analytic
continuation to τ = it, but in most of the development below this specification is
not necessary; we generally will regard τ as a complex number. Then

                            Ô(x, τ ) = eĤτ Ô(x) e−Ĥτ .                        (24.3)

By construction, the field operators satisfy Heisenberg ”equation of motion”
                             ∂
                                Ô(x, τ ) = [Ĥ, Ô(x, τ )]                       (24.4)
                             ∂τ
In fact, most of the calculations can be done at real τ (”imaginary time”). In that
case the ”time ordering” Tτ means that the operators in (24.2) are ordered according
decreasing values of τ - the operators with greater τ are placed to the left.

                                            254
    We have derived this relation in quantum mechanics (Lecture 7), and then as-
sumed that it can be extended to QFT. In fact, such extension is not straightforward,
and involves many subtleties mostly related to regularization and renormalization
(Our brief inspection of the Wilson’s RG gives the idea of the size of the prob-
lem). Here I would like to revisit the relation (24.2) from more general perspec-
tive. Starting from the functional integral definition of the correlation functions, we
will try to recover basic notions of the operator formalism. Eq.(24.3) involves the
Hamiltonian, and we would like to see how the Hamiltonian, or more generally, the
Energy-Momentum P µ , can be introduced in terms of the correlation functions.

Correlation Functions and Energy-Momentum
Consider the correlation function

                                   ⟨ T µν (x) O1 (x1 )...ON (xN ) ⟩

involving the energy-momentum tensor field T µν (x). As we have seen in the previous
Lecture, it satisfies the Ward Identity (23.9), in particular

               ⟨∂µ T µν (x) O1 (x1 ) · · · ON (xN )⟩ = 0 for x ̸= x1 , · · · , xN ,

As a consequence, the integral
                      Z
                          dΣµ (x) ⟨ T µν (x) O1 (x1 )...ON (xN ) ⟩
                            Σ

taken over a d − 1-hypersurface Σ does not change under topologically trivial de-
formations of Σ. The hypersurface Σ divides the space Rd into two parts, and
correspondingly splits the set of insertion points x1 , ..., xN into two subsets, accord-
ing to their locations within the parts. Topologically trivial are those deformations
which do not change this splitting, or, in other words the deformations which do not
make the hypersurface to ”cross over” any of the insertion points. For the sake of
our present discussion it is convenient to choose take Σ to be an ”equal-time slice”.
As before, we nominate one of the coordinates in Rd to be the (imaginary) ”time”
τ i.e. x = (x, τ ), and define Σ(τ ) as the union of points with given τ . Define37

Taking µ to be the ”time” index τ , and recalling that P τ = iP 0 = iH, one recovers
(24.4)39 . The ”spatial” components of this equation

                                 i∇O(x, τ ) = [P, O(x, τ )]

reproduce the well known (see e.g. Chapter 2 of PS) representation of the Heisenberg
field operators
                       Ô(x, t) = e−iPx+iHt Ô(0, 0) eiPx−iHt
which I chose here to write directly in Minkowski space-time.

Space of States40
The functional integral is particularly suitable for dealing with the correlation func-
tions (24.1). Calculations in the previous paragraphs suggest a natural way to con-
struct the space of states H and an algebra of operators, in terms of the Euclidean
space correlation functions, as follows.
    Let us again split the whole space Rd , with the coordinates xµ = (x, τ ), into two
half-spaces
                                     R = Rd+ ∪ Rd−
separated by the ”equal time” slice, the hypersurface τ = 0. Namely

                                     Rd+ :     (x, τ ), τ ≥ 0 ,
                                     Rd− :     (x, τ ), τ ≤ 0 ,

We associate states with the product of field insertions φ(x1 )...φ(xM ) at the points

                                    x1 , x2 , ..., xN ∈ Rd− ,
  39
    sign
  40
    The ideas displayed in this section are borrowed from the construction in K. Osterwalder, R.
Schrader, ”Axioms for Euclidean Greens functions,” Comm. Math. Phys. 31 (1973), 83-112; 42
(1975), 281-305.


                                               259
                       φ(x1 )...φ(xM ) → | φ(x1 )...φ(xM ) ⟩ .                     (24.13)

At this point, the above equation just provides notation for the states. We define
H as the linear envelope of the above states

                     H = Span | φ(x1 )...φ(xM ) ⟩ : xi ∈ Rd−
                                

    The intuition behind this construction comes from the simple minded idea of H
as the space of functionals

                                   H = Space of Ψ[φ(x)]

of the field configurations φ(x), x ∈ Rd−1 . Let xi = (xi , τi ) ∈ R− , and consider the
functional integral
                    Z
                               [Dφ(x)] φ(x1 )...φ(xM ) e−A[φ(x)]                 (24.14)
                     φ(x,0)=φ(x)

over all field configurations φ(x) in the half-space x ∈ R− . This integral defines (up
to overall normalization) the functional

                                    Ψφ(x1 )...φ(xM ) [φ(x)]

which may be regarded as the Schroedinger wave function representing the state
(24.13). In particular, the integral (24.14) with no insertions represents the vacuum
state,                     Z
              Ψ0 [φ(x)] =              [Dφ(x)] e−A[φ(x)] → | 0⟩ ∈ H .
                            φ(x,0)=φ(x)

  The problem with this representation is in the definition of the scalar products.
The simple minded definition
                              Z
                 ⟨Ψ2 | Ψ1 ⟩ := [Dφ(x)] Ψ∗2 [φ(x)]Ψ1 [φ(x)]                  (24.15)

has all required formal properties, but involves the functional integration which, as
we already know, requires regularization and renormalization. In this context the
problem of renormalization looks much harder then that in the functional integral
(24.1) because the functionals Ψ[φ(x)] do not have (quasi) local structure, and
generally are far more complicated then e−A .
   Instead, one defines the scalar product of the states (24.13) as

    ⟨φ(xM +1 )...φ(xN ) | φ(x1 )...φ(xM )⟩ := ⟨φ(x̄M +1 )...φ(x̄N )φ(x1 )...φ(xM )⟩ (24.16)

                                             260
Here the ”mirror image” points x̄ are defined as follows:

                     ”Reflection” :           x = (x, τ ) ↔ x̄ = (x, −τ )                      (24.17)

Then, if all the points x1 , ..., xM , y1 , ..., yN in (24.16) are in Rd− , the ”mirror” points
ȳ1 , ..., ȳN are all in Rd+ . The configuration of the points in the correlation function
in the r.h.s. of (24.16) is exactly as in (24.9), see Fig.25.1 above.
    The most important property of the scalar product is its positivity,

                                              ⟨Ψ | Ψ⟩ > 0

for any | Ψ⟩ ∈ H, | Ψ⟩ =    ̸ 0. For the formal representation (24.16) the positivity
is obvious: Eq.(24.15) with Ψ1 = Ψ2 is explicitly positive. For the scalar prod-
uct defined in terms of the correlation functions this property is postulated as the
reflection positivity
     XZ
                  ⟨φ(ȳ1 )...φ(ȳN )φ(x1 )...φ(xM )⟩ fN∗ (y1 , ..., yN )fM (x1 , ..., xM ) ≥ 0
     N,M    xi ,yk ∈Rd−


for any sequence of functions fN (x1 , ..., xN ), which expresses positivity of the norm
of the state            XZ
                 | Ψ⟩ =            fN (x1 , ..., xN ) | φ(x1 )...φ(xN )⟩ .
                               N    xi ∈Rd−


    More generally, we can associate states with general products of local fields
Oa (x),

    Oa1 (x1 )...OaN (xN )     → | Oa1 (x1 )...OaN (xN )⟩ ∈ H ,             x1 , ..., xN ∈ Rd− (24.18)

according to the same idea
                                         Z
         ΨO1 (x1 )...ON (xN ) [φ(x)] =                 [Dφ(x)] O1 (x1 )...ON (xN ) e−A[φ(x)]
                                         φ(x,0)=φ(x)

with the scalar product product related to their correlation functions similarly,

⟨Ob1 (y1 )...ObM (yM ) | Oa1 (x1 )...φ(xM )⟩ := ⟨Ōb1 (ȳ1 )...ŌbM (ȳM )Oa1 (x1 )...OaN (xN )⟩

where again x1 , ...xN ; y1 , ..., yM ∈ Rd− , and the points ȳi ∈ Rd+ are the ”mirror
images” of yi , as in Eq.(24.17). However, when the fields Oi (xi ) are not all scalars,
the fields Ōb are related to Ob by certain ”reflection”. To get an idea assume that the

                                                  261
field O(x) is real-valued vector with the components Oµ (x). Then, if µ = 1, 2, ..., d−1
represent ”spatial” components,

                        Ōµ (x̄) = Oµ (x̄) ,    Ōτ (x̄) = −Oτ (x̄) ,

i.e. the sign of the ”temporal” component Oτ gets flipped upon reflection. For
example,
                               ∂τ φ(x) = −∂τ φ(x)
so that
                        ⟨∂ν φ(y)|∂µ φ(x)⟩ = ± ⟨∂ν φ(ȳ)∂µ φ(x)⟩
where the sign + applies when both µ and ν are ”spatial” or both are ”temporal”,
otherwise the sign is −. Similar rule applies to higher rank tensors. For complex
values fields the ”reflection” involves complex conjugation. The correlation func-
tions of composite fields Oa obey then the suitably generalized reflection positivity
conditions, which follow from the positivity of the norms of the states (24.18).
    As an elementary application, let us show that in a scale invariant (i.e. RG fixed
point) theory the mass dimensions Dα of all nontrivial fields Φα (x) are positive (we
denote Φα (x) the scalar fields that diagonalize the matrix of anomalous dimensions,
see Lecture 20). From the scale invariance
                                                         Cα
                             ⟨Φα (x)Φα (y)⟩ =
                                                     |x − y|2Dα

where |x − y| is the Euclidean distance between the points x and y. Let us take
τ > 0, so that x = (x, −τ /2) ∈ Rd− . Set y = x̄ = (x, τ /2). We have

                                                         Cα
                              ⟨Φα (x) | Φα (x)⟩ =               .
                                                       (τ 2 )Dα
The positivity requires Cα > 0; One can set Cα = 1 by adjusting normalization of
Φα . Consider the norm
                                                         
                         ∂                 ∂
                            Φα (x, −τ /2)     Φα (x, τ /2)               (24.19)
                        ∂x1               ∂x1

where x1 is one of the spatial coordinates in x = (x1 , x2 , ..., xd−1 ). This norm is
expressed as the second derivative of the correlation function

                             ∂2
                                    ⟨Φα (x, τ /2)Φα (y, −τ /2)⟩
                           ∂x1 ∂y 1

                                               262
evaluated at y = x. Elementary calculation yields for (24.19)

                                         2Dα
                                         2
                                       (τ )Dα +1

which must be positive, hence Dα ≥ 0.
     The case D = 0 is instructive. If the field Φ has mass dimension D = 0 it follows
that
                                        ∂
                                           Φ(x) = 0 .
                                       ∂x1
It is then easy to prove that any correlation function involving the derivative of Φ(x)
vanishes
                  ⟨∂µ Φ(x) O1 (x1 )...ON (xN )⟩ = 0 , ⇒ ∂µ Φ(x) ≃ 0 .
Then one can show (using cluster factorization, see below) that

                   ⟨Φ(x) O1 (x1 )...ON (xN )⟩ = ⟨O1 (x1 )...ON (xN )⟩

i.e. the only field with zero mass dimension is the identity operator.
