25       Energy-Momentum Operators
The expression (24.9) can be now promoted to the definition of the operators P̂ µ
acting in the space H. Specifically, for any x1 , ..., xN , y1 , ...yM in Rd− define

     ⟨φ(y1 )...φ(yM ) | P̂ µ | φ(x1 )...φ(xN )⟩ = ⟨φ(ȳ1 )...φ(ȳM ) P µ φ(x1 )...φ(xN )⟩ (25.1)

where the correlation function in the r.h.s. involves the integral of the energy-
momentum tensor over the equal-time slice τ = 0,
                   Z                       Z
        µ                      µν
      P (τ ) := −i    dΣµ (x) T (x) = −i          dd−1 x T µτ (x, τ = 0) , (25.2)
                        Σ                           Σ(τ =0)

It is straightforward to check, using the definition of the inner product, that the
”spatial” components P = (P 1 , ..., P d−1 ) are Hermitian, while P τ is anti-Hermitian.
We therefore define d Hermitian operators (P̂, Ĥ) as
                                      Z
                             k
                           P̂ := −i             dd−1 x T kτ (x, τ ) ,             (25.3)
                                        Σ(τ =0)
                                   Z
                           Ĥ = −            dd−1 x T τ τ (x, τ ) .               (25.4)
                                        Σ(τ =0)


Obviously, Ĥ coincides with P̂ 0 := −iP̂ τ , the real-time component of P̂ µ .
    A remark is in order. If we are dealing with the field theory in the infinite space-
time Rd , the matrix elements of these operators diverge because of the contribution
of generally nonzero expectation value

                                      ⟨T µν (x)⟩ = g µν ε

where g µν − δ µν is the Euclidean metric tensor, and ε is the vacuum energy density.
It is convenient to get rid of this divergence by defining reduced energy-momentum
tensor
                               T µν (x) → T µν (x) − g µν ε
which has zero expectation value. This can be interpreted as adding the ”cosmolog-
ical term” to the action
                                                √
                                         Z
                            A → A + ε dd x g(x)

For the fixed metric background such term has no effect on the content of the theory,
but brings in the above subtraction when T µν (x) is defined as the response to the

                                              264
variation of the background metric,
                              1         √
                                 Z
                       δA = −       dd x g T µν (x) δgµν (x) .
                              2
In what follows we adopt this convention, assuming that T µν (x) is chosen in such a
way that

                                     ⟨T µν (x)⟩ = 0 .                                 (25.5)


    With this convention, it is easy to prove that the vacuum | 0⟩ is the eigenstate
of P̂ µ with the eigenvalue 0, i.e.

                             Ĥ | 0⟩ = 0 ,        P̂ | 0⟩ = 0 .                       (25.6)

Indeed, consider the element

               ⟨O1 (x1 )...ON (xN ) | P̂ µ | 0⟩ = ⟨O1 (x̄1 )...ON (x̄N ) P µ ⟩        (25.7)

where P µ is the surface integral (25.5). As all insertions x̄1 , ..., x̄N are in Rd+ , the
hypersurface Σ involved in the definition (25.1) can be deformed away to the domain
τ → ∞, where the integral vanishes. As the result, all matrix elements (25.7) are
equal to zero. Eq.(25.6) follows.
    Similar calculation shows that the operators Pµ act on the states O1 (x1 )...ON (xN )⟩
as follows
                                             " N       #
                                              X ∂
              P̂µ | O1 (x1 )...ON (xN )⟩ = i         µ   | O1 (x1 )...ON (xN )⟩
                                              k=1
                                                  ∂x k

Indeed, this time deforming Σ to the τ → −∞ domain leaves behind integrals
around the insertion points xk , which, according to
                                   Z

                                                265
Stationary States. Energy Positivity
The operators P̂ µ commute, and thus can be simultaneously diagonalized in H. It
is convenient to discuss the spectrum of the Hermitian operators P̂ µ , µ = 0, ..., d − 1
with P̂ 0 := −iP̂ τ = Ĥ, which, of course, coincide with the conventional Energy-
Momentum operators in the Minkowski space-time. Let | α⟩ be an eigenstate

                                   P̂ µ | α⟩ = pµα | α⟩ .

    We will make few assumptions about the spectrum of these operators. One is
about the spectrum of Ĥ. We assume that the eigenvalues Eα of Ĥ are satisfy
Eα ≥ 0, with the only zero eigenvalue E0 = 0 being associated with the vacuum
state, Eq.(25.6). This assumption implies

                                lim e−LĤ =| 0⟩⟨0 |                                (25.9)
                                L→∞

    There are several simple consequences from this condition alone. One is the so-
called cluster factorization property of the correlation functions, which states that
if a collection of N points consists of two groups with very large separation between
the groups, correlations between the fields in the different groups tend to zero, that
is

            lim ⟨O1 (x1 )...OM (xM )OM +1 (xM + L)...ON (xN + L)⟩ =
           L→∞
                          ⟨O1 (x1 )...OM (xM )⟩⟨OM +1 (xM )...ON (xN )⟩           (25.10)

To prove, take the direction of L to be the ”time”, then the l.h.s. can be represented
as
                 ⟨O1 (x1 )...OM (xM ) | e−LĤ | OM +1 (xM )...ON (xN )⟩
and (25.10) follows from (25.9).
    Less obvious consequence concerns analytic properties of the correlation func-
tions
                             ⟨O1 (x1 , τ1 )...ON (xN , τN )⟩
at complex values of τ1 , ..., τN . We have briefly studied this question at the beginning
of the course, for the case of the Klein-Gordon propagator (Lecture 4). In fact,
our arguments there depend only on the condition of the positivity of the energy
spectrum, which was explicit in the KG theory. Therefore, the analysis can be
repeated word for word in general case. Let me restate the result for the two point
function
                                     ⟨O1 (x, τ )O2 (0, 0)⟩

                                           266
In the complex plane of t = −iτ we have

                                    ⟨0 | Ô2 (0, 0) Ô1 (x, t) | 0⟩

                                                                                 C+
                −|x |                                            +|x|
                                       CT
  C_
                               ⟨0 | Ô1 (x, t) Ô2 (0, 0) | 0⟩

Here
                         Ô(x, t) = e−iPx+iHt Ô(0, 0) eiPx−iHt
are conventional Heisenberg field operators. The discontinuity across the branch
cuts represents the commutators which lay within the future and past light cones
(”local commutativity”).

Spectrum of Energy-Momentum
Some properties of the spectrum of the operator P̂ µ = (Ĥ, P̂) can be established
on general grounds. Let P be the spectrum of this operator, i.e. the set of its
eigenvalues

          pµ ∈ P ⇒ ∃ | α⟩ ∈ H such that pµ = pµα :               P̂ µ |α⟩ = pµα |α⟩ .

   Lorentz invariance suggests that

                               pµ ∈ P ⇒ Λµν pν ∈ P ,

where Λ is a Lorenz transformation. This means that any pµ ∈ P such that pµ pµ =
m2 enters P together with the entire hyperboloid

                        Pm2 = {pµ : pµ pµ = (p0 )2 − p2 = m2 } .

It follows then from the positivity of the energy that P lays entirely within the
future light cone pµ pµ > 0, p0 > 0.
    Another important property of P is its additivity, i.e.

                             pµα , pµβ ∈ P ⇒ pµα + pµβ ∈ P .

                                            267
Intuitively, if we think of the state | α⟩ carrying the momentum pµα as a local ex-
citations over the ground state, it is possible to add another excitation, with the
momentum pµβ arbitrary far away from the first one. At large separations the exci-
tations do not interact, and their energies and momenta add up. Formal derivation
using the cluster property will be considered later.
   The additivity implies that if P contains Pm2 , it also contains all pµ of the form

                          pµ = pµ1 + · · · + pµN ;    pµi ∈ Pm2 .

The totality of such vectors pµ will be denoted P̄m2 ,

            P̄m2 = {pµ = ∪N ∪p1 ,··· ,pN {pµ = pµ1 + · · · + pµN ; pµi ∈ Pm2 } .

The set P̄m2 looks like this
                                          E−E0

                                                                    | p 1p 2p 3>

                                                            | p 1p2>

                                                        |p>
                                                                        |p|

    Note that P̄m2 is exactly the spectrum of P̂ µ in the Klein-Gordon fields theory,
where we have found that
                                         d3 p µ †
                                     Z
                                 µ
                               P̂ =            p ap ap ,
                                        (2π)3
                             p
where pµ = (ωp , p), ωp = p2 + m2 , and ap , a†p are particle creation and anni-
hilation operators. There these states are interpreted as N -particle states of free
bosons.
    The spectrum P̄m2 in some sense is the minimal Lorentz-invariant set compatible
with the above additivity condition. If this spectrum appears in an interacting QFT
we say that the theory contains just one massive particle of the mass m. The states
associated with the hyperboloid Pm2 are the one-particle states, and the states with

                                             268
(pµ )2 ≥ 4m2 are interpreted as the scattering states of two or more interaction
particles.
    In general case the spectrum can be more complicated (even in a scalar QFT
like we consider here). It can involve more then one kind of stable particles, and
hence would contain several hyperboloids, say Pm21 and Pm22 , and correspondingly
its continuous part would look more complicated (as the drawing shows).




   In any case, if we form the operator
                                       P̂ 2 = Ĥ 2 − P̂2 ,
the discrete components of its spectrum are associated with the one-particle states,
and the continuous spectrum incorporates all the multi-particle scattering states.
   ”Asymptotic completeness”: P = ∪a Pm2a
